It also doesn't touch `san-miguel-form-options.ts` (7n already flagged barangay/nature-of-business lists as real per-municipality reference data needing its own data model, still untouched).

**Still not done, on purpose — not automated by this feature**: no wildcard Vercel domain has been set up. Each new client's own public-facing subdomain (for applicant branding on `/apply`) still needs the same manual "add domain in Vercel + CNAME in GoDaddy" step done for `portal.muniserve.ph` (7l) — `/admin`'s footer says so explicitly. Staff can sign in immediately via the shared login regardless of that step's status.

**Follow-up, same day — full client-data access for troubleshooting.** The project owner asked directly whether the platform admin could open any client's dashboard to troubleshoot, without ever being added to that client's own staff roster (now or in the future). Given the choice between read-only and full BPLO-equivalent access, the project owner chose full access.

The chosen mechanism is deliberately **not** a parallel admin-only access path bolted onto every client-data table — it's a single reusable `staff_users` "proxy" row per platform admin (`staff_users.is_admin_proxy`, migration `0019_platform_admin_view_as.sql`). From `/admin`, a "View as" control per client lets the admin pick a target role (BPLO, Treasury, Mayor, or a specific department, encoded as `department:<name>` in one `<select>` since this plain-forms page has no client JS for a conditional second dropdown) — `viewAsLgu` (`src/app/admin/actions.ts`) then upserts that one row's `lgu_id`/`role`/`department` and redirects to `/dashboard`.

Because this row carries the platform admin's own real `auth_user_id` and a real `lgu_id`, **every existing staff-scoped RLS policy already grants it exactly the access a genuine staff member at that LGU would have** — applications, businesses, payments, permits, department_reviews, fee lines, all of it, with zero new policies needed on any of those tables. This is why it's one reusable row rather than one row per (LGU, role) combination: `getCurrentStaff()` (`src/lib/staff.ts`) does `.eq("auth_user_id", ...).maybeSingle()`, which would throw on ambiguity if a platform admin ever accumulated more than one `staff_users` row — upserting the same row on every "View as" click keeps that invariant intact by construction, and also means `dashboard/page.tsx`'s router (reordered to check `getCurrentStaff()` *before* `getCurrentPlatformAdmin()`) sends a platform admin who has picked a target straight into that role's real dashboard, identically to genuine staff, only falling back to `/admin` when no "View as" target has been picked yet.

Three places needed to explicitly exclude `is_admin_proxy` rows rather than silently mis-treating them as real client staff: `/dashboard/staff`'s roster (so it doesn't clutter or become deactivatable), `setStaffActive`'s "last active BPLO" guard (so a real client's last BPLO can't be deactivated just because an admin happens to be viewing that LGU as BPLO at the same moment), and `review-workflow.ts`'s department-rejection BPLO email fan-out (the proxy's email is a synthetic `admin-proxy+<id>@internal.muniserve.ph` address that's never meant to actually receive mail). `dashboard/layout.tsx` shows a banner ("🛡️ Platform Admin — viewing X as Y") on every page under `/dashboard` whenever the signed-in staff identity is a proxy row, specifically so a platform admin can never mistake a client's real dashboard for their own account, and every action taken while proxied is attributed to `"<admin name> (Platform Admin)"` in whatever `staff_users.full_name` join displays it (reviewer names, override attribution, etc.) — never silently as if it were that client's own staff.

**Follow-up, same day — pausing and deleting a client.** The project owner asked directly for two more client-lifecycle actions on `/admin`: pausing a client (e.g. hasn't paid yet) and deleting one outright.

**Pause** (`lgus.is_paused`/`paused_at`, migration `0020_lgu_pause.sql`) doesn't touch any of the client's data — `setLguPaused` just flips the flag. The actual enforcement lives entirely in `dashboard/layout.tsx`, the one chokepoint every dashboard route already passes through: when the signed-in staff identity's own LGU is paused, it renders a full-page `PausedNotice` ("Your account is currently Paused, please contact your administrator for more info," with `hello@muniserve.ph` / `0977-440-1374`) instead of `{children}` — no individual dashboard page needed its own check, since a paused staff member can never reach a page with action buttons on it regardless of which URL they land on. A platform admin's "View as" proxy row is deliberately exempt from this check, since they still need to be able to open a paused client's dashboard to check on it (e.g. to verify payment before resuming).

**Follow-up, same day — pausing the public applicant side too.** The first pass deliberately scoped Pause to staff sign-in only, flagged plainly rather than silently assumed, and offered to extend it. The project owner said yes. `apply/page.tsx` already gets `isPaused` for free from `resolveLguDisplay()` (added to `LguDisplay` in the same migration) — when true, it renders `ApplyPausedNotice` instead of `ApplyPageClient`, so a paused client's applicants never see the wizard at all. `submit-application/route.ts` also rejects outright (`isLguPaused(lguId)`, a new lightweight lgu.ts helper) as defense in depth, for a tab already open before the pause took effect or a direct call to the route.

**Deliberately different copy from the staff-facing notice**: `ApplyPausedNotice` says "Applications ... are temporarily unavailable. Please visit the [BPLO office] in person, or check back later" — no mention of "Paused," no `hello@muniserve.ph`/phone number. The staff notice is about that LGU's own relationship with MuniServe (e.g. an unpaid invoice); an applicant has no part in that and shouldn't be shown it or given a channel to contact MuniServe about it. `/status/[reference]` (checking an already-submitted application) is untouched — pausing blocks *new* submissions, not visibility into work already in progress.

**Delete** (`deleteLguClient`) is real, not theatre, but deliberately limited: it only succeeds when the LGU has **zero** `applications` and **zero** `businesses` on file. This isn't an arbitrary restriction — `payments`, `permits`, `documents`, and `notifications_log` all key off `applications.id` with no `on delete cascade`, so a `lgus` row with any real application history would fail a raw delete with a foreign-key violation anyway (checked directly against the schema before writing this, not assumed); forcing it through by adding cascades later would silently destroy real payment/permit records, exactly what this schema's soft-delete convention (7m) exists to prevent. A client with real data should be paused, never deleted. `/admin` shows each client's application/business counts so the admin knows why Delete isn't offered before they try, and when it is offered, requires typing the LGU's exact name to confirm — this page has no client JS for a real `confirm()` dialog, and deleting a client warrants deliberate friction over a single accidental click.

**Follow-up, same day — making the applicant form's picklists multi-LGU.** 7n flagged this and deliberately left it unfixed ("if MuniServe ever needs a second LGU with its own picklists, this is the file to turn into a per-LGU lookup") since MuniServe was still single-tenant in practice at the time. Now that a real second client is actually being onboarded, this is that pass: `src/lib/san-miguel-form-options.ts`'s hardcoded `BARANGAY_OPTIONS`/`NATURE_OF_BUSINESS_OPTIONS` are no longer the runtime source for the applicant form.

New `lgu_form_options` table (migration `0021_lgu_form_options.sql`) — one shape (`lgu_id`, `option_type` in `'barangay'`/`'nature_of_business'`, `value`, `sort_order`) covers both, rather than two tables, so a third picklist type could be added later without another migration. Seeded with San Miguel's existing 49 barangays and 220 nature-of-business options via `supabase/seed/seed_lgu_form_options.mjs` (Node + service-role, same pattern as `import_permit_history.mjs` — not because 269 rows is large, but because hand-typing that many literal business/barangay names through the SQL editor's Monaco instance risks a transcription error the script can't make; verified programmatically against the original file's arrays before running, not just eyeballed). `getLguFormOptions()` (`src/lib/lgu-form-options.ts`) is the new runtime source, called from `apply/page.tsx` alongside `resolveLguDisplay()` and passed down to `ApplyPageClient` as a `formOptions` prop.

**The two option types get deliberately different empty-list behavior, not the same fallback treatment:**
- **Barangay: no fallback.** A barangay list has no sensible default across LGUs — showing San Miguel's real barangay names to a different municipality's citizens would be actively wrong, not just generic (exactly the bug this pass exists to fix). When a client has zero barangay rows, `ApplyPageClient`'s `withDynamicOptions()` degrades that one field from a dropdown to free text rather than showing an empty or wrong list. `/admin`'s create-client form gained an optional "Barangays (comma-separated)" field, mirroring the existing departments field's UX, so a platform admin can set this at onboarding without raw SQL — but it's optional, matching this whole session's "don't block onboarding on a detail that isn't always available yet" pattern.
- **Nature-of-business: falls back to San Miguel's list.** Unlike barangays, a generic Philippine business-type taxonomy is a reasonable out-of-the-box default for any new client, so `getLguFormOptions()` returns San Miguel's list when a client hasn't set their own — no onboarding step required, the form just works. This default isn't cosmetic-only: `application-form-logic.ts`'s conditional show/hide rules (e.g. `natureOfBusiness === "Billiard Hall"` → show `billiardTableCount`) are hardcoded against these exact strings, sourced from San Miguel's own real live form (7d). Using the same default list means those rules keep working correctly for any new client — but a client that later customizes their own nature-of-business wording would silently stop matching those specific rules. Flagged plainly rather than hidden: genuinely generalizing that conditional-logic engine per client would mean rebuilding 7d's whole extraction process (get that client's own real live form, never guess) for every onboarding — real, but explicitly out of scope for this pass, which is about the picklist *values* being correct per client, not re-deriving each client's entire form structure.

`fieldLabel()`'s static `BUSINESS_INFO_FIELDS`/`ADDRESS_FIELDS` arrays in `ApplyPageClient.tsx` are untouched (a field's label never varies by LGU) — only the rendered field list is rebuilt per-render via `withDynamicOptions()`, swapping in `formOptions`'s values for exactly the `natureOfBusiness`/`barangay` entries.

**Follow-up, same day — telling the BPLO where their own public form actually is.** The project owner asked directly: once a client is onboarded, how does their BPLO actually find the link to embed on their website or share with applicants? Nothing in the product surfaced it anywhere — a real gap, not a hypothetical, since `<subdomain>.muniserve.ph/apply` is easy to guess but never actually shown to anyone.

Fixed in three places, not just one, since a single email is easy to lose:
- **The welcome email** (`createLguClient`) now includes the client's own `https://<subdomain>.muniserve.ph/apply` link alongside the existing shared `/login` link, with a plain note that it won't resolve until this subdomain's DNS/Vercel setup (7l) is actually done.
- **`/admin`'s client list** shows the same link as a clickable `↗` next to each client (replacing the old plain-text subdomain label), with a one-line note underneath explaining it's the link to give the BPLO and where else it's shown.
- **`/dashboard/staff`** (BPLO-only) shows it persistently as its own card, so it's discoverable inside the product itself at any time, not just recoverable from an old email.

All three surfaces use the exact same URL shape and the same caveat wording (works only once the domain's own DNS/Vercel setup is done) — deliberately, so there's one consistent story about this link everywhere it appears, not three slightly different explanations.

**Follow-up, same day — an iframe embed code so applicants never see the muniserve.ph URL.** The project owner asked directly: what if an LGU wants to embed the form on their own website without applicants seeing `<subdomain>.muniserve.ph`?

**Framing wasn't actually restricted anywhere before this** — `next.config.ts` had no `headers()` config at all, meaning every page in the app, including `/dashboard` and `/admin`, was embeddable by any external site with zero restriction (a real clickjacking exposure on authenticated staff pages that simply hadn't come up before nothing needed embedding). Fixed properly rather than just opened up `/apply`: `next.config.ts` now sets `Content-Security-Policy: frame-ancestors 'self'` by default on every route, with an override to `frame-ancestors *` on `/apply` specifically — the only route with a real reason to be embedded by an arbitrary third-party domain, since there's no fixed allowlist of client LGU websites to restrict it to. `X-Frame-Options` is deliberately not set alongside this — CSP's `frame-ancestors` supersedes it, and setting both risks inconsistent enforcement across browsers that disagree on which one wins.

**Auto-resize, not a fixed-height iframe.** The wizard's real height varies enormously between screens (a short landing screen vs. the ~40-field full form), so a fixed iframe height would either clip content or force an ugly double-scrollbar. `ApplyPageClient` now observes its own root container with a `ResizeObserver` and posts `{source: "muniserve-apply", height}` to `window.parent` via `postMessage` on every height change — harmless no-op when the page isn't actually embedded, since `window.parent === window` in that case and nothing listens for the message. `buildApplyEmbedSnippet()` (`src/lib/embed.ts`) generates the paired `<iframe>` + a small listener script that resizes the frame to match, checking `event.origin` against that LGU's own domain before trusting a resize message (the embedding page could contain other frames).

**Surfaced next to the plain link** (7o's earlier follow-up) rather than a separate new location: a new shared `EmbedCodeBox` component (`src/components/embed-code-box.tsx`, textarea + copy button, self-contained inline styles so it works identically on Tailwind-styled dashboard pages and the plain-styled `/admin`) sits right next to the plain apply link both on `/admin` (collapsed behind a native `<details>` disclosure, since that page lists every client and an always-expanded snippet per row would make the list unwieldy) and — as of the very next follow-up below — `/dashboard/settings` rather than `/dashboard/staff`, where it was first placed before the project owner asked for it to move.

**Follow-up, same day — Audit Trail and Performance Stats.** The project owner asked directly for a full audit/activity trail, explicitly with DILG compliance in mind ("this is one of the things that the DILG would love to see"), plus a second, related feature: a report showing "the speed of that municipality in terms of handling business applications... where mostly the department that takes time approving, or where the bottleneck is happening."

**Audit trail data model**: a new, append-only `audit_log` table (migration `0022_audit_log.sql`) rather than relying solely on the audit columns already scattered across individual tables (`applications.initial_review_by/at`, `department_reviews.reviewer_id/reviewed_at/notes`, `payments.received_by`, `permits.issued_at`, etc.). Those columns are untouched and stay the actual source of truth for the workflow itself — `audit_log` is a parallel, denormalized, unified feed built specifically for chronological review/reporting/export, which those scattered columns can't provide directly without hand-joining six tables every time. `actor_label` is captured as plain text at the moment of the event (e.g. "Juan Dela Cruz (BPLO)"), not looked up live via a join, so the trail stays accurate even if the acting staff account is later renamed or deactivated — the whole point of an audit trail is that it doesn't silently rewrite history when something about the actor changes later.

**Instrumented everywhere a real state change happens**: `bplo/actions.ts` (initial review, assessment finalized, printed, released), `review-workflow.ts`'s `submitDepartmentDecision` (every department decision, not just rejections — tagged with `acted_on_behalf` when BPLO acts for a department per rule #9), `treasury/actions.ts` (payment recorded), `mayor/actions.ts` (permit signed), `businesses/actions.ts`'s walk-in flow, `staff/actions.ts` (added/activated/deactivated), and `admin/actions.ts` (client onboarded, paused, resumed) — the last three because a gap in a client's own activity history (e.g. "why did nothing happen for three weeks") is exactly the kind of thing this needs to explain, not just workflow-internal events. Logging is best-effort, same reasoning as `src/lib/notifications.ts`: the real state change has already succeeded by the time `logAuditEvent()` is called, and a logging failure must never roll back or block it.

**Closed one real gap** found while designing this: there was no captured timestamp for "BPLO finished the fee assessment" (the `pending_bplo_assessment` → `pending_payment` transition) — every other stage transition already had one. Added `applications.assessment_finalized_at` in the same migration, set in `finalizeAssessment`.

**Who can see it — BPLO and Mayor, not every role.** The project owner's own answer, when asked: "BPLO Only and Mayor should see this." Consistent with rule #8 (a department shouldn't see another department's queue) — a full activity feed necessarily crosses every department at once, so it can't be opened to department/treasury roles the same way; BPLO already sees across every department by design (rule #9), and Mayor is the LGU's own final authority, so both get read access via a `bplo`/`mayor`-scoped RLS SELECT policy. INSERT is open to any staff role at their own LGU (treasury/mayor/department all log their own real actions).

**`/dashboard/audit`** shows the full LGU-wide feed (last 90 days by default, widened via date pickers), searchable and filterable by action type, with CSV export — same client-side search/filter/export pattern as Permit History's own table (7f). Deliberately **no separate per-application inline "Activity" section** on the review cards themselves — `bplo/page.tsx` already has several different card renderers across its queues, and every logged event's summary consistently includes the application's own reference number, so searching this one page for e.g. "MS-2026-00001" already answers "what happened to this specific application" without duplicating a timeline UI across every card type. Flagged as a scope decision, not silently skipped.

**`/dashboard/stats`** (Performance Stats) computes, in `src/lib/performance-stats.ts`: overall and by-type (new/renewal) average/median days from submission to release; a per-stage average-duration breakdown (initial review, departments, assessment, payment, printing, mayor's signature, release); department turnaround ranked slowest-first (the direct answer to "where's the bottleneck," with the slowest department called out explicitly); and a live "currently stuck" table ranking open applications by how long they've sat at their current stage. All durations are computed in JS from plain fetched rows (matching this app's established pattern in `business-status.ts`/`fee-engine.ts`, not hand-rolled SQL aggregation) — the module's own comments document which stage durations are exact (e.g. payment: `assessment_finalized_at` → first payment) versus best-effort approximations given what's actually captured (e.g. "days stuck" for `pending_bplo_assessment` falls back to `submitted_at`, since there's no captured "departments just cleared" event separate from the department_reviews rows themselves). No chart library — simple CSS width-scaled horizontal bars, matching the icons' own "hand-authored, no external dependency" approach.

**Both new pages reached via real nav pills** ("Audit Trail", "Performance Stats"), not icons like Settings — these are primary reporting sections a BPLO or Mayor would check regularly or hand to DILG, not occasional admin tasks. `DashboardTopBar` gained `auditHref`/`statsHref` props, gated to `bplo`/`mayor` at the same six call sites `staffHref`/`settingsHref` already touch (with department/treasury pages correctly left unchanged, matching the access decision).

**Follow-up, 2026-08-14 (after first real use) — the flat feed was the wrong shape.** Feedback after actually looking at `/dashboard/audit`, from a screenshot of the shipped page: "it shows the log like the time, actor, action but to whom does it belong? Which business was it? I was thinking more of a list of applications, then when clicked, it will show the entire history." A fair miss — the original design's own reasoning ("every summary includes the reference number, so search already answers this") assumed a reader would search for a specific application; a reader scanning the feed cold has no such query yet, and a business's identity was never a column at all, only buried in free-text `summary` strings.

Rebuilt `audit-trail-table.tsx` as `AuditTrailExplorer` (`page.tsx` now also fetches `applications`/`businesses` for every `application_id` referenced in the selected date range, since `audit_log` itself never stored those as columns — see migration 0022's comment on why `actor_label` is denormalized text but `application_id` stays a real FK): a "By application" tab groups every event by `application_id` into one row each (reference no., business name, type, current status via the same `TonePill`/status-label convention as Businesses/Permit History, last-activity time, event count) — click a row to expand that one application's own events inline, oldest-first, as a timeline (reusing `WorkflowStepper` at the top of the expansion so the reader also sees where it currently sits in the 8-stage pipeline, not just its past). A second "Other activity" tab keeps the old flat-list shape for the events that have no `application_id` at all (staff/account changes) — nothing to group those under, so splitting them out rather than mixing two different shapes in one table. CSV export exists at both levels: the top-level button still exports everything in range (now with each row's reference number resolved as its own column instead of only living inside `summary`'s text), and each expanded application also gets its own single-application export, for handing just one business's history to someone without the rest of the LGU's activity in the file.

Verified visually before shipping (not just type-checked) via a temporary unauthenticated route with fixture data — `/dashboard/*` is gated by `src/lib/supabase/middleware.ts`'s proxy before any page code runs, so a real login was the only other way to see it render — deleted immediately after confirming the expand/collapse and both tabs worked.

**Follow-up, same day — a dedicated Settings page, not the Staff page.** The project owner pushed back on where the previous follow-up put the apply-link/embed-code card: "the entire 'Your public application form' does not belong inside the staff tab" — and flagged that more LGU-level settings are coming, so this needed a real home, not a one-off relocation.

New `/dashboard/settings` (BPLO-only, same guard as `/dashboard/staff`) now owns the "Your public application form" card (plain link + `EmbedCodeBox`), moved out of the Staff page entirely. Reached via a new gear icon in `DashboardTopBar` (`settingsHref` prop, gated identically to `staffHref` — same four call sites: `bplo/page.tsx`, `businesses/page.tsx`, `businesses/history/page.tsx`, `staff/page.tsx`, all conditioned on `staff.role === "bplo"`) rather than a text nav pill like Applications/Businesses/Staff, since it's a shortcut into LGU-level configuration rather than a primary day-to-day section. Built as a real page, not a modal or inline section, specifically because the project owner said outright there's more to add here later — the next LGU-level setting has an obvious home instead of needing another "where does this belong" decision.

**Follow-up, same day — the first BPLO account is optional on client creation.** `createLguClient`'s `bploEmail` field is no longer required. The project owner's real workflow: a new client is often created and test-driven via "View as" before their actual BPLO email is known, sometimes not until physically at that LGU's office — requiring it up front would block onboarding on a detail that isn't always available yet. When left blank, no `staff_users` row or welcome email is created at all; the real first BPLO gets added later the same way anyone else does, from `/dashboard/staff` — reachable in the meantime via "View as BPLO" for that LGU, so nothing is blocked either way.

**A real bug caught immediately on first live use, not by reasoning about it in advance**: clicking "View as" for `info.muniserve@gmail.com` (the platform admin) always bounced straight back to `/admin`, no matter which role/client was picked. Root cause: that same Google account already had a **genuine** San Miguel BPLO `staff_users` row from earlier testing, so the moment "View as" created its proxy row, `getCurrentStaff()`'s `.eq("auth_user_id", ...).maybeSingle()` (`src/lib/staff.ts`) started matching *two* rows for that one `auth_user_id` — a case the original design never accounted for (it assumed a platform admin would have zero pre-existing staff rows). PostgREST's `.maybeSingle()` errors on more than one row, `getCurrentStaff()` treated that error as "not staff," and `dashboard/page.tsx`'s router fell through to the platform-admin check and sent them back to `/admin` — silently, with no error shown. Fixed by having `getCurrentStaff()` fetch every row for that `auth_user_id` and pick deterministically: an *active* admin-proxy row always wins over a real account, so "View as" reliably takes effect even when the same login is also genuine client staff somewhere. Added `exitViewAs` (deactivates the proxy row, never deletes it) and an "Exit view-as" button in the dashboard banner specifically for this case — without it, that one account would have had no way back to using its own real BPLO account normally.

---

## 7p. LBT-category dead end, closed at the source (2026-08-14)

Caught by the project owner live-testing a walk-in filing ("Mags Poultry Farm"): all 5 departments approved, the application correctly advanced to Assessment review — and immediately hit `fee-engine.ts`'s "no LBT category set" block, with the message's own "Open the Business Registry" link leading to a Directory expand-row that had nothing editable in it. A genuine dead end, not just a rough edge: once an application passed initial review, there was **no UI path anywhere** to set `businesses.lbt_category`, because the only control for it (`bplo/actions.ts`'s `setLbtCategory`, on `InitialReviewCard`) only renders while an application is still at `pending_bplo_initial` — and the walk-in flow (7e) skips that stage entirely, so a walk-in filing could never reach that control even once. The project owner's own diagnosis was exactly right: "before the application moves to the department review, the LBT should be selected already and should not move if not."

**Root cause was structural, not a validation gap** — there are exactly two places in the codebase that can put an application into `pending_dept_review` (`submitInitialReview` and `startWalkInApplication`, confirmed by grepping every write of that status string), and neither one checked for an LBT category before doing so.

**Fixed at the shared model layer first** (`src/lib/lbt-categories.ts`, already the one module owning "what LBT categories exist" — extended rather than duplicated elsewhere, matching the SOLID-principles instruction given this same day): `setBusinessLbtCategory()` is now the one function that writes `businesses.lbt_category` (both `bplo/actions.ts`'s and the new `businesses/actions.ts`'s controls call it, instead of each running its own `.update()`), and `requireLbtCategorySet()` is the shared gate, called from both `submitInitialReview` and `startWalkInApplication` right before either can advance to `pending_dept_review` — the walk-in path checks it before any side effect (owner creation, reference number, application insert), not after.

**Every call site gets a real, disableable UI, not just a server throw**: the gate functions throw (mirroring `finalizeAssessment`'s existing `if (!result.ok) throw new Error(...)` pattern) as a defense-in-depth backstop, but the primary guard is the button itself going inert. `DecisionButtons` (`ui.tsx`) gained a `disableApprove` prop — `InitialReviewCard` passes it whenever `!profile?.lbtCategory`, greying out only Approve/Approve-with-condition (Request info/Reject stay clickable, since neither needs a category) alongside a warning banner explaining why. The walk-in form's own submit button in the Business Registry gets the identical treatment. `btnBase`/`MiniButton` needed a `disabled:` variant added to their Tailwind classes — neither had one before, since nothing in this app disabled a button conditionally until now.

**Closed the actual dead end, not just its cause going forward**: the Business Registry (`businesses/page.tsx`) gets its own LBT-category editor (a new `setLbtCategory` action in `businesses/actions.ts`, reusing the shared writer) — always visible to BPLO regardless of a business's status, including `in_progress`, specifically because that's the one status the walk-in form itself hides behind a "see the Applications tab" note. This is also what makes `fee-engine.ts`'s existing "Open the Business Registry" message actually true for the first time — that link pointed nowhere fixable before this. Unblocking the already-stuck "Mags Poultry Farm" test case needs no data migration: `AssessmentCard` already computes live on every render, so setting the category via this new control is enough on its own.

Verified with `tsc`/a full build plus a temporary unauthenticated fixture route exercising the real exported `ui.tsx` components (`DecisionButtons`, `PrimaryButton`) with the same props the real pages now pass — confirmed via computed styles (`opacity`, `pointer-events`, the `disabled` DOM property) that Approve/Approve-with-condition go inert while Request info/Reject/Save stay clickable, and that the normal (category-set) case is fully unaffected — deleted immediately after.

---

## 7q. `owners` had RLS enabled with zero policies -- a real gap since migration 0002 (2026-08-14)

Traced from the same "Mags Poultry Farm" test as 7p: after the LBT-category fix, the application correctly reached and passed Assessment -- but the project owner then asked why it showed "Owner: Unknown applicant" when the applicant (Benj Maglente) had submitted through the real online form with a complete profile.

**Confirmed directly against production** (read-only queries first, via the newly-connected Supabase MCP): the data was completely correct -- `businesses.owner_id` pointed at a real `owners` row with `full_name`/`phone` both populated, and the application's `initial_review_notes` was null (not the walk-in flow's synthetic note), confirming this went through the real applicant-facing form, not a walk-in filing. The identical embedded query BPLO's dashboard runs returned the owner correctly under the service-role key, but not under a normal staff session.

**Root cause**: `owners` has had RLS enabled since migration 0001/0002, alongside every other staff-visible table -- but unlike `businesses`, `applications`, `payments`, `permits`, etc., it was never actually given a policy. Postgres denies by default with RLS on and no policy, and PostgREST's embedded joins (`owner:owners(...)`) return `null` for a blocked row rather than erroring the whole query -- which is exactly why this went unnoticed for so long. It degrades silently to a plausible-looking fallback ("Unknown applicant"/"Unknown owner") instead of a visible crash.

**Not just a display bug** -- the same missing policy affects every staff-scoped embedded `owner:owners(...)` join in the codebase: owner names across every dashboard (BPLO/Businesses/Department/Mayor/Treasury), applicant SMS phone lookups fired from staff actions (`bplo/actions.ts`, `treasury/actions.ts`), the owner/representative name printed on generated permit PDFs (`mayor/actions.ts`'s `signPermit`), and BPLO's walk-in claim-by-phone flow (`businesses/actions.ts`) -- both finding an existing owner by phone and creating a new one were silently broken by the identical gap.

**Fixed** (migration `0023_owners_rls_policy.sql`): a SELECT policy scoped the same "own LGU" way as every other table lacking its own `lgu_id` column (`application_fee_lines`/`review_rounds`/`payments`/`permits`/`documents`/`notifications_log`'s existing pattern from migration 0002) -- staff can see an owner only if that owner has at least one business at their own LGU, via `exists (select 1 from businesses b where b.owner_id = owners.id and b.lgu_id = ...)`. Plus an INSERT policy for BPLO's walk-in owner-claim, gated on role alone since `owners` has no `lgu_id` column to check in `WITH CHECK` (owners can genuinely span LGUs by phone, per section 5's identity model -- this is deliberate, not an oversight).

**One known, narrow, unfixed edge case, flagged rather than guessed at**: if a walk-in's phone number belongs to an owner who only has businesses at a *different* LGU, the SELECT policy still won't surface them to this LGU's staff, so the insert will hit `owners.phone`'s unique constraint and throw -- a loud, visible error for BPLO to notice and escalate, not silent duplication, so left as-is rather than widening owner visibility across LGU boundaries for a rare case.

**Applied directly against production** via the newly-connected Supabase MCP server (`apply_migration`), not just written and handed off -- verified by re-running the exact query BPLO's dashboard runs, inside a transaction with `set local role authenticated` + `request.jwt.claims` set to the real staff member's `auth_user_id`, confirming it now returns the owner correctly under RLS rather than under the service-role bypass used for the initial diagnosis. `get_advisors` (security) also re-run afterward: `owners` no longer appears in the "RLS enabled, no policy" findings.

**Follow-up, same day — the rest of the security-advisor scan.** The project owner asked to check the other findings `get_advisors` surfaced alongside 7q's fix, not just the one that broke something visibly.

- **`applicant_sessions`/`application_reference_counters`/`otp_codes` -- RLS enabled, no policy (INFO): left alone, confirmed by design.** Traced every caller first: all three are touched exclusively via `createServiceClient()` (session tokens, OTP codes) or only indirectly through `generate_application_reference()` (the counter table) -- never through a staff or anon session. "No policy" here means "nobody but service-role can read this," which is the correct posture for OTP codes and session tokens, not a gap like `owners` was.
- **`current_staff()`/`generate_application_reference()` callable by `anon` (WARN): real, fixed.** Both are SECURITY DEFINER functions exposed over PostgREST's public `/rest/v1/rpc/` endpoint. Traced every real caller before touching anything: both are only ever invoked server-side (service-role from the applicant route, or an authenticated BPLO session from the walk-in flow) -- never via the anon key, and no browser code calls `.rpc()` at all. Most concrete risk: `generate_application_reference()` called anonymously with any `lgu_id`/`year` would burn through that LGU's real reference-number sequence with no application behind it.

  **First fix attempt (migration `0024`) turned out insufficient** -- verified directly against `information_schema.routine_privileges` rather than assuming a `revoke ... from anon` had worked, and it hadn't: Postgres grants `EXECUTE` to the `PUBLIC` pseudo-role by default on function creation, and `anon` inherits `PUBLIC` grants regardless of a revoke targeted at `anon` specifically. Migration `0025` corrects it: revoke from `PUBLIC`, then explicitly re-grant to `authenticated` (needed -- `current_staff()` runs inside RLS `USING` clauses across nearly every policy in this schema, evaluated as the querying role for a real staff session) and `service_role`. Re-verified with the same privilege query plus a fresh `get_advisors` pass: both `anon`-callable warnings are gone; the `authenticated`-callable versions of the same two warnings remain, expected and left as-is since that access is load-bearing.
- **`auth_leaked_password_protection` disabled (WARN): flagged, not fixed.** An Auth-service setting (HaveIBeenPwned check on new passwords), not a Postgres policy or grant -- no tool available in this session to toggle it. Left for the project owner to enable directly in the Supabase Dashboard (Authentication → Policies → Password Security) if wanted; unrelated to anything else fixed this session.

Both migrations applied directly against production via the Supabase MCP connection (same session as 0023), same verify-before-trusting approach throughout: read the actual grant state before and after each change rather than assuming the SQL did what its name implied.

---

## 7r. Fee engine generalized: fee shapes, regulatory fees, Automated Assessment (2026-08-14, same day)

The project owner asked for four things after seeing the live assessment card: rename the Mayor's Permit/LBT lines to fixed generic labels, reorder them, add a Settings-configurable "Regulatory Fee Flat Amounts" section (CNC, Health Permit Fee, Inspection Fee, Plate Fee, Regulatory Fee, Sanitary Fee -- always included, LGU-specific amounts), and floated making LBT-category rates Settings-configurable too, explicitly to make onboarding new clients easier. Talked through the design before building anything, per the project owner's own request -- two real revenue-code documents (a full 2006 San Ildefonso Revenue Code spreadsheet with a "2026 Proposed" rate column, and San Miguel's own already-scanned ordinance, both actually read in full, not skimmed) changed the plan materially before a line of code was written:

- **San Ildefonso's Mayor's Permit Fee is structured completely differently from San Miguel's.** San Miguel: a catalog of ~110 specific named business types + a standard fallback tiered by prior-year LBT. San Ildefonso: no per-name catalog at all -- a business-size tier (Cottage/Small/Medium/Large, the national MSME asset-value-or-employee-count classification, "whichever yields the higher fee") crossed with ~6 coarse categories. Confirmed directly from each LGU's own ordinance text, not assumed.
- **Sanitary Fee and Garbage Fee (San Ildefonso) aren't flat either** -- graduated by floor-area bracket, and Garbage Fee also varies by business category. Directly relevant to the "Regulatory Fee Flat Amounts" ask: building that as flat-only would have silently misassessed any LGU shaped like this one.
- The project owner's own response to that finding, unprompted: a Settings toggle to switch off automated computation entirely for a given LGU, falling back to hand-entered amounts for Local Business Tax and Mayor's Permit Fee (generalized during the conversation to "any non-flat shape," not just those two by name) -- "a safe place to go if whatever we design fails."

**Schema (migrations `0026`/`0027`)**: `fee_rules.fee_category` (`mayors_permit`/`lbt`/`cedula`/`regulatory`/`discount`) replaces the old name-sniffing matching logic (`name.startsWith("LBT Schedule")`, `computation_type === "formula_increment"` meaning CEDULA) with an explicit discriminator -- backfilled against San Miguel's real 119 rows using the exact logic being replaced, verified by count before and after. A new `tier_matrix` computation shape (San Ildefonso's Mayor's Permit structure) reuses `fee_rule_brackets` with a new `tier_label` column instead of a min/max range, rather than a parallel table -- one bracket-editor component can eventually serve both shapes. `lgus.automated_assessment_enabled` (default true) is the toggle itself. `application_fee_lines` gained `fee_category`/`display_label`/`is_manual`, denormalized at write time (same reasoning as `audit_log.actor_label`, migration 0022) -- a manually-entered line has no single `fee_rules` row to point at, and a report reading a past assessment needs to show what was actually charged and labeled *then*, not whatever `fee_rules` looks like if it's edited later.

**Engine (`fee-engine.ts`)**: every existing computation path (bracket lookup, the new-business flat-rate-with-floor rule, the Mayor's Permit catalog + standard fallback, CEDULA, the essential-commodity discount) is untouched in logic, only in how it identifies which rows to look at -- verified directly, not just by inspection: ran the real `computeApplicationFees()` against a live San Miguel application (Mags Poultry Farm, MS-2026-00003) both before and after, byte-identical output (₱10,000 / ₱500 / ₱1,005, total ₱10,500). New: a `regulatory` loop that includes every active row unconditionally (or, if several rows share a name with distinct `applies_to` categories, matches like Mayor's Permit's catalog does -- Garbage-Fee-shaped); `tier_matrix` resolution (asset value from `capitalInvestment` on a `new` application -- there's no captured current-asset figure for a `renewal`, so that case falls back to employee count alone with a note flagging the approximation, same pattern as the existing `preceding_year_lbt_paid` gap). One real bug caught by testing the new shapes against synthetic San-Ildefonso-like data (not just eyeballing the code): a category-split regulatory fee with only one configured category variant was being treated as unconditional instead of gated by that category -- fixed and reverified before shipping.

**A real, previously-dormant bug surfaced, deliberately not silently fixed**: San Miguel's seed script has always included an "Application Fee" row (₱10, flat, `applies_to = 'all'`) that the *old* engine never actually matched or charged -- a real, harmless-but-real gap, confirmed by checking production directly. The new `fee_category` backfill kept it bucketed as `mayors_permit` (preserving its exact current dormant behavior, matching this pass's own goal of reproducing San Miguel's numbers exactly) rather than moving it to `regulatory` and silently starting to charge it. Flagged for the project owner to decide -- not fixed either way without asking, since it's a real (if tiny) change to what gets charged going forward.

**Settings UI**: a new "Regulatory Fee Flat Amounts" card (name + amount + online/counter, add/deactivate -- soft-delete only, matching this schema's standing convention) and an "Automated Assessment" toggle, both BPLO-only. New RLS (migration `0027`): BPLO can manage `fee_rules` scoped specifically to `fee_category = 'regulatory'` (not opened more broadly -- LBT schedules and the Mayor's Permit catalog/tier-matrix stay a service-role/migration task for now, see below) and can update their own LGU's row on `lgus` (same "RLS bounds rows, not columns" caveat as `setStaffActive`, migration 0015 -- the action code only ever writes the one intended column).

**Assessment card**: fixed category order and labels (Mayor's Permit Fee, Local Business Tax, regulatory fees, CEDULA last) regardless of which specific rule matched. When Automated Assessment is off, `AssessmentManualSection` (`bplo/assessment-manual-fields.tsx`, a client component -- the one piece of this whole pass that needed real interactivity, since the project owner asked for the total to update live as BPLO types) swaps in hand-entered fields for every manual-eligible line, pre-filled with whatever the engine did manage to compute as a starting point. Mandatory: `finalizeAssessment` requires Local Business Tax and Mayor's Permit Fee to be manually entered whenever the toggle is off, even if the engine found nothing to compute at all (a genuinely blocked result no longer hard-stops the whole card in manual mode -- fixed a related bug found along the way: a missing capital-investment/gross-sales figure used to abort the *entire* computation, which would have also silently hidden the always-included regulatory fees; now it only blocks the one LBT line).

**Deliberately not built in this pass, stated plainly rather than silently skipped**: a self-service bracket-table/tier-matrix editor UI, so a BPLO could type in a new LBT schedule or Mayor's Permit tier grid without a migration. The *engine* fully supports both shapes now (verified against synthetic San-Ildefonso-shaped data, including the tier_matrix path and a category-split graduated regulatory fee) -- onboarding a new LGU's actual tax rates still goes through a migration/seed script for now, same process as every LGU onboarded so far (read the actual ordinance, never a derived summary alone, per the standing rule from 7b). San Ildefonso itself was **not** created as a real client/LGU row in production during this pass -- verification used the real exported `computeApplicationFees()` function against synthetic data matching its ordinance, not a persistent second tenant nobody had asked to actually onboard.

---

## 7s. Business Tax & Mayor's Permit Fee Setup: CSV import (2026-08-15)

7r's own "deliberately not built" note flagged the real gap directly: the engine could handle multiple LGU shapes, but onboarding a new client's actual tax rates still meant a developer reading the ordinance and hand-writing a seed script or raw SQL run through the Supabase MCP connection -- a real bottleneck the moment more than one or two clients need onboarding. The project owner asked directly for a scalable fix, floated a CSV-upload idea, and this section is the design discussion and the build that followed, informed by two real ordinances read in full: San Miguel's (already on file) and a newly-provided **2006 San Ildefonso Revenue Code** spreadsheet.

**What the discussion settled on, and why it's safe:** the risky step was never "who clicks upload" -- it's "who correctly translates a real ordinance's prose brackets into structured numbers." That step doesn't change: a developer (via Claude Code) still reads the actual ordinance and produces the filled file, exactly like it always has. What changes is that the *output* is a reviewable spreadsheet uploaded through the app instead of raw SQL run directly against the database, which adds a real safety property that didn't exist before -- the file itself is inspectable before it goes live, and the upload flow shows a plain-language preview of every rule before anything is written.

**Two templates** (`src/lib/fee-rule-import.ts`), matching `fee_category`:
- **LBT**: always the graduated-bracket shape (`computation_type = 'tiered'`). One row per bracket (schedule name, category code, optional new-business flat-rate-with-floor %, then min/max/base fee/rate %/rate basis). Confirmed against San Ildefonso's real Retailer schedule that a pure flat-percentage-with-one-threshold schedule (2.2% up to ₱400k, 1.1% above, no real bracket table in the ordinance at all) still fits cleanly as two brackets with `rate_basis = 'full_amount'` -- no third shape needed.
- **Mayor's Permit**: a `shape` column per row (`flat`, `per_unit`, `tier_matrix`, or the `standard_new`/`standard_renewal` fallback) covers both San Miguel's named-business-type catalog and San Ildefonso's business-size tier grid in one file format. A file mixing `tier_matrix` rows with `flat`/`per_unit` rows is rejected outright -- one LGU only ever uses one shape, per both real ordinances read.

**Deliberately out of scope for v1, stated plainly rather than silently unsupported**: San Ildefonso's per-vehicle-type flat rates with no basis at all (public utility permit fees), and a Mayor's Permit `standard:renewal` fallback that's itself tiered by prior-year LBT (San Miguel's actual shape) -- importing that one specific row still needs a migration. Every other row in both real ordinances read for this pass imports cleanly.

**Round-trips both ways, on purpose**: downloading a template exports the LGU's *currently active* rates in the same shape the upload expects, not a blank form -- a brand-new LGU with nothing set up yet gets a labeled example to replace, an LGU updating next year's numbers gets *their own* rates back as an editable starting point. Both "set this up for the first time" and "change a couple of numbers" are the same download-edit-upload loop.

**Preview before publish**: upload parses the file and shows a plain-language line per rule (e.g. *"Security Agency (applies_to "security agency") -- ₱300 base + ₱50 per locality count"*) plus any warnings, before anything touches live data. Only an explicit "Looks right -- publish" click writes anything.

**Publish re-parses server-side**, never trusts the browser's own preview state -- same reasoning as `finalizeAssessment`'s "never trusts the page's own preview, re-runs computation server-side," since a client value is technically editable. New rules are inserted first; only once every insert succeeds does the fee category's previously-active rules get deactivated (soft-delete, matching this schema's standing convention everywhere else) -- not a real database transaction (supabase-js has no multi-statement transaction API against PostgREST), but bounded so a failed upload can't leave an LGU half-covered by old rates and half by a broken new set: on any insert failure, whatever new rows *did* get inserted this run are deleted again and the old rules are never touched.

**Migration `0028_bplo_lbt_mayors_permit_rls.sql`** widens migration 0027's "BPLO can manage regulatory fees" RLS pattern to `fee_category in ('lbt', 'mayors_permit')` on both `fee_rules` and `fee_rule_brackets` -- deliberately left service-role-only in 7r ("stays a service-role/migration task for now"); this pass is what changes that, since the new preview-before-publish flow is what makes opening it up safe. Applying this migration to production was blocked by the session's own auto-mode permission classifier (a direct schema/policy write) -- applied only after the project owner explicitly approved that specific action, not worked around.

**A real latent bug fixed along the way**: `getLbtCategoryOptions()` (`src/lib/lbt-categories.ts`, powers BPLO's manual LBT-category dropdown) filtered on `name LIKE 'LBT Schedule%'` -- a naming-convention guess that would have silently hidden any imported schedule not named that exact way. Switched to `fee_category = 'lbt'` (migration 0026's real discriminator, the same fix this project already applied to the fee engine itself) -- a strict widening, every existing row already satisfies both, verified by construction.

**Verified against real data, not just synthetic scenarios**: San Miguel's live Schedule A shape, both Mayor's Permit styles (including per-unit Security Agency and the standard fallback), and San Ildefonso's actual tier_matrix rows straight out of the source workbook (including a category with only Medium/Large tiers, not all four) all round-trip correctly through parse/build/summarize. Also confirmed the template-download route fails closed (403) with no staff session, and checked the component's real rendering against the app's actual design system via a temporary unauthenticated fixture route, deleted immediately after -- same technique 7o/7p already established for verifying without a real Google-OAuth staff login, since neither that nor a real file-picker upload can be driven from this environment (the same file-picker limitation flagged back in 7d).

**Follow-up (2026-08-15, later that same day) -- a real bug caught by the project owner's own first live use, not by testing.** The project owner ran the CSV-conversion prompt (see 7z) against San Miguel's real ordinance scan and tried uploading the resulting `lbt_schedules.csv` -- nearly every row failed with "schedule_name and category_code are both required." Root cause: `parseLbtCsv` required `schedule_name` on *every* row, but the documented convention this whole feature was built around -- and what `buildLbtTemplateCsv` itself already produces when it writes `new_business_rate_percent`/`delivery_mode` blank after a schedule's first row -- says `schedule_name` (like those two) only needs to be on the first row of each schedule. The uploaded file was built exactly as documented and still got rejected; the parser's own validation was stricter than its own contract. Fixed: `category_code` is now the only thing required on every row (it's the real grouping key); `schedule_name` is only required the first time a `category_code` appears, and a blank `schedule_name` on a later row of the same schedule is treated as expected, not an error -- the mismatch warning now only fires when a later row provides a *different* `schedule_name`, an actual conflict. Verified directly against the exact shape the onboarding prompt (7z) documents, plus a round-trip of what `buildLbtTemplateCsv` itself downloads, plus confirmed a genuinely missing `category_code` is still correctly rejected.

---

## 7t. Settings reorganization: staff management moved in, sections reordered (2026-08-15, same day)

The project owner asked directly to declutter the top nav and consolidate: staff-account management (`/dashboard/staff`, its own tab since 7m) moved into `/dashboard/settings` as a new first section, **"Add/Remove Staff"**, and "Your public application form" (Settings' original first section, since 7o) moved to the bottom.

**Why staff management fits here rather than its own tab**: it's an occasional admin task -- add/deactivate an account -- in the same category as the rest of Settings (fee setup, regulatory fees, the Automated Assessment toggle), not a primary day-to-day section like Applications or Businesses. The reordering itself (public application form to the bottom) was the project owner's own explicit instruction, not a judgment call made here.

**Structure mirrors the 7s pattern deliberately**: `src/app/dashboard/settings/staff-management.tsx` (presentational, a plain server component -- every control is already a server-action form, nothing needed client state) and `staff-actions.ts` (server actions, moved from `dashboard/staff/actions.ts` with `revalidatePath` updated to `/dashboard/settings`), same one-file-per-section split `fee-rule-import.tsx` already established rather than growing `settings/page.tsx` or `settings/actions.ts` into a single large file.

`src/app/dashboard/staff/` is deleted entirely -- no redirect left behind, since nothing else in the app links to that path anymore (checked every reference, including the welcome-email copy in `admin/actions.ts` and the doc comments in `admin/page.tsx`/`embed-code-box.tsx`/`auth/callback/route.ts`, all updated to point at `/dashboard/settings`). `DashboardTopBar` (`ui.tsx`) drops the `staffHref` prop and the "Staff" nav pill entirely, and `"staff"` is removed from the `active` tab union -- every call site that used to pass `staffHref` (bplo, businesses, businesses/history, audit, stats) updated accordingly.

Verified with a full `next build` (not just `tsc --noEmit`, which fights with Next's own generated ambient types once `.next` is cleared) -- clean, and `/dashboard/staff` no longer appears in the route list.

---

## 7u. `current_staff()` picking the wrong staff_users row -- a schema-wide RLS bug, not a Treasury bug (2026-08-15, same day)

Live-testing Treasury: entering an OR number and clicking "Record payment" threw a bare server error every time. `get_runtime_errors` (Vercel) named the real cause immediately: `42501 new row violates row-level security policy for table "payments"` -- not a bug in `recordPayment()` (`treasury/actions.ts`) at all, which matched CLAUDE.md's own spec (advances `pending_payment -> pending_printing`, SMS's the applicant "your permit is now being printed") exactly.

**Root cause, confirmed directly against production before writing a fix**: `current_staff()` -- the SQL function nearly every RLS policy in this schema calls, not just payments' -- was `select * from staff_users where auth_user_id = auth.uid() limit 1`, with no ordering at all. A platform admin who has ever used "View as" (migration 0019, section 7o) legitimately owns *two* `staff_users` rows for one login when that Google account is also real client staff somewhere -- exactly the project owner's own test account, confirmed with a query: one inactive real `bplo` row (left over from earlier testing), one active admin-proxy row currently set to whatever role "View as" last picked. With no `order by`, Postgres was free to return either row, and reliably returned the wrong one -- so `role = 'treasury'` in the payments INSERT policy evaluated false every time, regardless of which role the proxy row actually claimed to be.

**This was already fixed once, but only at the app layer.** `src/lib/staff.ts`'s `getCurrentStaff()` solved this identical problem back in section 7o's own "View as" work -- "the active admin-proxy row always wins over a real account" -- after the *exact same account* got silently bounced back to `/admin` for the exact same reason. That fix never propagated down to the SQL-level `current_staff()`, so every RLS policy in the schema (`applications`, `businesses`, `payments`, `permits`, `department_reviews`, `fee_rules`, `lgus`, `staff_users`, `audit_log`, ...) stayed exposed to the same nondeterminism this whole time -- the identical *class* of bug as 7q's "Unknown applicant" gap (app-layer and DB-layer logic silently drifting apart), just not yet tripped over a second table.

**Fixed** (migration `0029_fix_current_staff_row_selection.sql`): `current_staff()` now orders by the identical precedence `getCurrentStaff()` already uses -- an active admin-proxy row first, else the active real row. Applied directly to production, then verified two ways before calling it done, not just written and handed off: re-ran `current_staff()` under the exact failing account's session inside a transaction (`set local role authenticated` + `request.jwt.claims`) and confirmed it now resolves to the proxy row instead of the stale real one; then re-ran the actual blocked `payments` INSERT the same way, inside a rolled-back transaction, and confirmed it now succeeds.

**Worth remembering**: any staff-facing permission fix that touches `getCurrentStaff()` (app layer) needs the same check asked of `current_staff()` (DB layer) too, and vice versa -- this is now the second real bug from exactly that gap (7q, this one). The two are meant to answer the identical question ("who is this session, really") and have no business disagreeing.

---

## 7v. BPLO can record a payment on Treasury's behalf (2026-08-15, same day)

A real workflow gap, flagged by the project owner live-testing: "some applicants could walk on to the treasurer, pay the amount, get issued with receipt and goes back directly to the BPLO to show it. BPLO should also be able to approve it on Treasurer's behalf." Before this, an application sitting at `pending_payment` had exactly one path forward -- a Treasury staff member, specifically, recording it from their own dashboard -- so a walk-in who already paid and brought their OR receipt straight to BPLO had no way to move their own application along without separately tracking down Treasury.

**Same shape as rule #9's existing precedent** (BPLO acting on a department's behalf), applied to Treasury: BPLO gains exactly what Treasury already had here -- confirm payment, record an OR number -- not the ability to adjust what's owed. Rule #7's actual restriction is untouched; `recordPayment()` still never writes to `application_fee_lines`.

**Migration `0030_bplo_record_payment_on_behalf.sql`**: an additive INSERT policy on `payments` scoped to `role = 'bplo'` + own LGU, sitting alongside the original Treasury-only policy from migration 0002 (Postgres ORs multiple permissive policies together, same pattern used throughout this schema). `recordPayment()` (`treasury/actions.ts`) now accepts either role; the audit log tags a BPLO-recorded payment distinctly ("on behalf of Treasury") since `payments` has no `acted_on_behalf` column the way `department_reviews` does -- `received_by` already points at the real actor's own `staff_users` row regardless, so nothing about who actually received the money is ambiguous.

**Extracted the queue itself, not just the permission**: the query + cards + form used to live inline in `treasury/page.tsx` only. Pulled into a new shared `src/app/dashboard/payment-queue.tsx` (`AwaitingPaymentSection`) so BPLO's dashboard renders the identical thing rather than a second near-copy -- same reasoning as `review-workflow.ts`'s `openDepartmentReviewRound` or `lbt-categories.ts`'s `setBusinessLbtCategory`. Each page keeps its own visibility policy: Treasury always shows the section (it's their one job), BPLO only shows it when there's actually something waiting (`awaitingPayment.length > 0`), matching the same hide-when-empty convention "Ready to print"/"Ready to release" already use for BPLO's own secondary confirm-only queues. Copy on BPLO's version says plainly this is normally Treasury's step, not the default path.

Verified directly against production, not just written and handed off: re-ran the exact blocked scenario as the real account role/LGU combination, inside a rolled-back transaction, and confirmed the payments INSERT now succeeds under BPLO's own session (the original Treasury-only policy is untouched and still passes its own case).

---

## 7w. Staff notifications: SMS + email on every new queue item (2026-08-15, same day)

The project owner flagged a real, asymmetric gap: MuniServe notified applicants at every point the ball moved into or out of their hands (section 7j), but staff had almost nothing -- only three touchpoints existed before this (staff invite/welcome emails, a department rejection/request-info alert to BPLO, and the 24-hour reminder cron), all email-only. A new application landing in BPLO's queue, a department round opening, all departments clearing, an assessment finalized, or an applicant uploading a document (the BFP payment-proof case from section 7c being the motivating one) all fired silently -- staff only found out by checking their own dashboard cold.

**The blocking gap, identified correctly by the project owner before any code was written**: `staff_users` had no phone column at all, so there was nowhere to send an SMS even if the rest of the plumbing existed.

**Scope discussed and confirmed explicitly, not assumed**: SMS on every one of these events, not just the highest-value few -- flagged directly as a real cost tradeoff (SMS is billed per message and this fans out to every department reviewer on every application), and the project owner chose the broad version anyway ("we can always change the volume of it in the future"). Recorded here so a future pass narrowing this isn't a mystery -- it's a deliberate, informed choice, not an oversight to fix.

**Migration `0031_staff_phone.sql`**: `staff_users.phone`, nullable and unvalidated at the schema level (same as `owners.phone` -- normalized app-side via the existing `normalizePhone()`, not a new convention). A staff member with no phone on file just never gets an SMS; email still always works, since it's their real login identity. No new RLS policy needed -- migration 0015's existing "bplo updates staff at their own lgu" UPDATE policy is already row-scoped, not column-scoped (the same "RLS bounds rows, not columns" pattern `setStaffActive` already relies on), so it covers writing this new column for free.

**UI**: an optional "Mobile (for SMS)" field on the "Add staff" form, plus the first real per-field edit control this page has ever had (`updateStaffPhone`, `staff-actions.ts`) -- an inline phone input + Save button on each roster row. Everything else here is still add-only plus activate/deactivate (7m's "not full profile editing" scope note stands); phone is the one exception, since it's the whole point of this pass.

**Infrastructure** (`src/lib/notifications.ts`): `notifyStaffSms` mirrors `notifyApplicantSms`'s best-effort/never-throws shape exactly. `notifyStaffByRole(lguId, role, applicationId, subject, emailHtml, smsMessage, department?)` is the new centralizing helper -- looks up every active, non-proxy staff member of a role (or a specific department) at an LGU and sends both channels, replacing what would otherwise have been eight near-identical hand-written lookups.

**Every wired trigger point**, each notifying whoever can actually act next:
- New application submitted (`submit-application/route.ts`) -> BPLO.
- A department review round opens, first time or resubmission (`review-workflow.ts`'s `openDepartmentReviewRound`, `bplo/actions.ts`'s `resubmitToDepartments`) -> that round's active department(s) only -- resubmission notifies just the re-triggered department(s), matching rule #6's existing targeting.
- All departments clear (`review-workflow.ts`'s `submitDepartmentDecision`) -> BPLO, ready for assessment.
- Assessment finalized (`bplo/actions.ts`'s `finalizeAssessment`) -> Treasury, payment due.
- Payment recorded (`treasury/actions.ts`'s `recordPayment`) -> BPLO, ready to print.
- Marked printed (`bplo/actions.ts`'s `markPrinted`) -> Mayor, ready to sign.
- Signed (`mayor/actions.ts`'s `signPermit`) -> BPLO, ready to release.
- An applicant uploads an additional document (`upload-additional-document/route.ts`) -> whoever can currently act, resolved from the application's own status: the department(s) still `pending` in the *most recent* review round if `pending_dept_review`, Treasury if `pending_payment`, BPLO otherwise (rule #9's existing cross-cutting visibility makes BPLO the correct fallback for every other stage, including `pending_bplo_initial`/`pending_bplo_assessment` and the post-assessment stages).

Deliberately unchanged: the existing 24-hour reminder cron stays email-only for now -- it's a different notification (stale/overdue, not "new item"), out of the scope the project owner actually asked for this pass.

Verified directly against production: confirmed the new `staff_users.phone` column and its RLS coverage, then ran `updateStaffPhone`'s exact write inside a rolled-back transaction under a real BPLO session and confirmed it succeeds.

**Follow-up, same day -- the "printed" notification to the Mayor was wrong, corrected the real workflow instead of just deleting it.** The project owner caught this immediately: at this pilot LGU, the Mayor never opens the app for a signature at all. The real physical process is BPLO prints the permit, personally carries it to the Mayor's office, gets it signed on paper, carries it back, and records the signature themselves -- so notifying the Mayor when something's printed would just be noise (they're about to be handed the physical copy by the person who'd also be reading that notification).

Two things followed from that, not just removing the notification:
- **`markPrinted` no longer notifies the Mayor at all** -- reverted, with a comment explaining why rather than silently deleting it (a future reader shouldn't have to guess whether this was an oversight).
- **BPLO can now sign a permit on the Mayor's behalf** (migration `0032_bplo_sign_permit_on_behalf.sql`) -- `signPermit` (`mayor/actions.ts`) accepts `role = 'bplo'` in addition to `'mayor'`, same "widen, don't replace" shape as migration 0030's payments-on-behalf and rule #9's department-decision-on-behalf. The Mayor's own dashboard path is untouched and still works identically for any LGU where a real Mayor login actually does this. Additive INSERT policies on `permits` and `permit_history` (both were previously `role = 'mayor'`-only, full stop) mirror the existing mayor-only ones rather than replacing them.
- **BPLO's dashboard gained a real action queue** for this stage ("At the Mayor's Office" -- printed and carried over, mark it once the signed copy comes back) instead of just the existing stat card with nothing underneath, same `Row` + single-button pattern as "Ready to print"/"Ready to release".
- **The applicant now hears "ready for pickup" the moment it's signed** (a new `notifyApplicantSms` in `signPermit`), not only once it's physically handed over (`markReleased`'s existing "released" SMS) -- these are two different, both-real moments now that signing and hand-off can be meaningfully separated in time (BPLO carrying it between two offices).
- **The BPLO "ready for release" staff notification only fires when the Mayor genuinely signed it themselves** (`actedOnBehalf` check) -- when BPLO is the one calling this on the Mayor's behalf, they already know it's done; notifying them of their own action would be pure noise, the same reasoning that started this whole follow-up.
- Audit log tags a BPLO-recorded signature distinctly ("(BPLO, on behalf of Mayor's Office)"), same convention as `recordPayment`'s "(on behalf of Treasury)" tag -- `permits` has no `acted_on_behalf` column the way `department_reviews` does.

Verified directly against production: both new INSERT policies confirmed present, then the exact `permits`/`permit_history` inserts `signPermit` performs re-run inside a rolled-back transaction under a real BPLO session -- both succeed.

---

## 7x. Print-ready certificate at "For Printing" (2026-08-15, same day)

The project owner flagged the actual remaining gap at this stage directly: "For Printing" was just a confirm button with nothing to print -- opening the card gave BPLO nothing they could actually print and carry to the Mayor. Floated a self-service uploadable-template idea; discussed the format options first (a fillable PDF with named form fields vs. an image with hand-specified coordinates vs. a Word/HTML template needing a document-conversion step), and recommended the fillable-PDF approach specifically because it needs zero new infrastructure -- `pdf-lib`, already the library generating the post-signing certificate (`permit-pdf.ts`, section 7k), has first-class support for reading and filling PDF form fields; a Word or HTML template would have reintroduced exactly the Puppeteer/LibreOffice server complexity 7k deliberately avoided the first time.

**Built the first template directly against a real reference**, not a guess: the project owner photographed their actual physical San Miguel Business Permit and shared it. Same standing rule as every other domain artifact in this project (fee rates, application form fields) -- read the real document, don't invent one. `src/lib/print-certificate.ts`'s `generatePrePrintCertificate()` matches that photo's actual field set, wording, and layout order: Permit No. + New/Renewal tag, Registered Trade Name, Business Address, Business Owner, Receipt No., Amount Paid, Issued On, the disclaimer paragraph with an embedded Valid Until date, the posting-requirement note, a blank signature line (no signature yet -- this is the pre-signature copy), the Mayor's name/title block, and a renewal-deadline paragraph.

**Explicitly not a pixel-exact recreation**, stated plainly rather than overclaimed: the real certificate's diagonal color-panel graphic design, embossed year watermark, and actual municipal/Philippine seal artwork are a real design asset that can't be reproduced with any fidelity from a phone photo. Reuses this codebase's own established visual system instead (permit-pdf.ts's navy-banner + label/value-row style) -- a reasonable first draft to adjust from, the identical framing 7k already used for the other certificate, not a final pixel-locked design.

**Deliberately genericized/omitted for multi-tenant safety, flagged rather than silently guessed** (same standing rule as 7b/7d/7h for fee rates and form fields -- never invent a legal specific for an LGU that hasn't confirmed it): the real reference cites San Miguel's own specific ordinance/resolution numbers (Ordinance No. 077, Resolution No. 92A-177) -- left out of the generic body text entirely, since those aren't safe to assume for a future second LGU. The January 20 renewal-deadline convention is kept (a real, common LGU pattern, not invented), with the year computed from the application year rather than hardcoded. The reference's own decorative footer (LGU tagline, Facebook page, specific contact email) is real marketing content for that one LGU, not copied -- a small MuniServe attribution line takes its place instead, matching `permit-pdf.ts`'s own footer convention.

**Deliberately kept separate from the existing post-signing PDF** (`permit-pdf.ts`'s `generatePermitAssets`, with the QR verification code) -- the project owner's own explicit call when asked directly. This one has no signature or QR code at all, since the permit isn't legally issued yet at "For Printing"; it exists purely as BPLO's own printable paper copy to carry to the Mayor for a wet signature.

**New `lgus.mayor_name`** (migration `0033_lgu_mayor_name.sql`) -- needed for the certificate's signature block ("HON. JOHN A. ALVAREZ" on the real reference), nowhere to store this before. No generic fallback exists for a person's actual name (unlike `bplo_office_name`'s reasonable English default) -- a client who hasn't set this yet just gets a blank signature block, not a guessed placeholder. Editable via a new "Permit Certificate Details" card on `/dashboard/settings` (`updateMayorName`, reusing migration 0027's existing "bplo can update their own lgu's settings" policy -- no new RLS needed, same "RLS bounds rows, not columns" convention as `setAutomatedAssessmentEnabled`). Seeded directly for San Miguel from the real reference photo ("John A. Alvarez") rather than left blank pending a manual edit, since the real value was already known.

**Served via a new route handler**, `/api/dashboard/print-permit` (BPLO-only, same gating as the rest of this queue) -- a route rather than a server action since a PDF response needs real `Content-Type`/`Content-Disposition` headers, which a server action's plain-JS-value return can't produce. `Content-Disposition: inline` so clicking "Open permit" on `bplo/page.tsx`'s "Ready to print" queue opens the PDF directly in a new tab, ready for the browser's own print dialog, rather than downloading a file first. Amount paid sums every `payments` row for the application (same pattern `signPermit` already uses for `amountPaid`); Receipt No./Issued On come from the most recent payment.

**A real bug caught by actually rendering a test PDF, not by reading the code and assuming it was right** (same verification discipline 7k's own "December 30 instead of December 31" bug was caught with): `pdf-lib`'s standard Helvetica font uses WinAnsi encoding, which has no glyph for the ₱ symbol (U+20B1) and throws outright rather than silently mangling it -- this had simply never been exercised before, since the existing post-signing PDF never draws a peso amount at all. Fixed by writing "Php" instead of "₱" for Amount Paid -- not a workaround-vs-ideal compromise, since the real reference certificate already writes "Php. 1,950.00" the same way, not the peso symbol.

**Deliberately not built in this pass, stated plainly**: the actual self-service "BPLO uploads their own template file" mechanism the project owner originally asked about. This pass ships the concrete first template (a real, working, from-scratch pdf-lib document matching the real reference's content) and the generation/serving pipeline around it; making the template itself swappable through a Settings upload -- the fuller vision from the original discussion -- is a natural next step, not done here.

---

## 7y. Self-service permit print template upload (2026-08-15, same day)

The actual self-service upload mechanism 7x deferred. The project owner asked directly whether a custom design (like the real reference photo, with its own color panels and seal artwork) could be preserved exactly, not just approximated -- an important question to answer honestly before building anything, since it determines the whole design.

**The real answer, confirmed before writing any code**: yes, but only if the uploaded file has actual fillable form fields in it (an AcroForm) -- `pdf-lib` can load an existing PDF's entire page content (every graphic, color, image) completely untouched and write text into named fields on top of it, without disturbing anything else. A flat image or a non-fillable scan (like the reference photo itself) has no such structure and can't be used directly -- there's nowhere for the system to know "put the permit number here specifically." This is exactly why 7x recommended the fillable-PDF format over an image-with-coordinates approach in the first place.

**Canonical field vocabulary** (`PRINT_TEMPLATE_FIELDS`, `src/lib/print-certificate.ts`): a fixed list of twelve data points a template's own fields can be mapped to -- permit number, application type, business name, business address, business owner, receipt no., amount paid, issued on, valid until, renewal deadline, mayor's name, mayor's title. Refactored `computeCertificateFieldValues()` out of `generatePrePrintCertificate()` (7x) so both the generated fallback and a custom-uploaded template compute the exact same formatted strings (same date formatting, same "Php" convention) from one place -- the two paths can't drift apart on formatting the way two independent implementations eventually would.

**Upload flow, same preview-before-publish safety shape as the fee-rule CSV import** (`src/lib/fee-rule-import.ts`): BPLO uploads a PDF, `previewPrintTemplate` reads back the file's own real field names (not a naming convention they have to guess) via `inspectPdfFields` (`src/lib/print-template-fill.ts`), and a mapping screen lets them choose, per field, which canonical data point fills it -- or leave it unmapped. Only text fields are offered as fillable (a checkbox/dropdown is reported back, not silently hidden, so BPLO can see *why* a field they expected isn't an option, rather than wondering if the upload failed). Nothing is saved until "Save template" is clicked; `publishPrintTemplate` re-reads the file's fields server-side and re-validates the mapping against them rather than trusting whatever the browser echoed back -- same reasoning as `finalizeAssessment`/the CSV import's own publish step.

**Storage**: new private `permit-print-templates` bucket (migration `0034_permit_print_template.sql`) -- same posture as `application-documents` (migration 0007), not `permit-pdfs` (migration 0014, public): this is an internal admin asset, not something that needs public read.
