## 7jj. Per-LGU SMS Sender Name, and a centralized brand prefix (2026-08-16)

The project owner flagged that every SMS starts with a hardcoded "MuniServe: " prefix and asked two things: should that change (maybe to "BPLO," maybe drop it entirely), and how would buying a custom Sender Name for an LGU actually work -- a separate API per LGU, or one API that knows which name to use? Researched the second question before answering either, since it's a real third-party API contract, not something to guess (this project's standing rule against guessing real-world facts) -- confirmed against Semaphore's own docs (semaphore.co/docs): one Semaphore account/API key can hold several approved Sender Names, and each `/api/v4/messages` call picks which one via a `sendername` parameter, defaulting to whichever Sender Name is marked default when omitted. So no second API key or account is needed per LGU -- Sender Names are a per-message parameter on the same account.

That answer shaped the recommendation for the first question too: keep the "MuniServe: " prefix for any LGU that hasn't bought/registered its own Sender Name (otherwise a message just arrives from generic "Semaphore" with no identifying text at all), but drop the prefix entirely once an LGU has one -- the Sender Name itself is what the recipient's phone shows as the "From," so a text prefix on top of it duplicates information that's already there. The project owner agreed; built as described.

**Migration `0040_lgu_sms_sender_name.sql`** (live in production): `lgus.sender_name`, nullable, no generic fallback -- same shape as `mayor_name`/`treasurer_name` (migrations 0033/0039). Records the exact Sender Name once purchased/approved directly with Semaphore; this migration/feature doesn't buy or register one itself. No new RLS policy needed -- migration 0027's general "bplo can update their own lgu's settings" policy already covers it (RLS bounds rows on `lgus`, not columns).

**`sendSms()` (`src/lib/semaphore.ts`)** gained an optional `senderName` parameter, mapped straight to Semaphore's own `sendername` field.

**Centralized the prefix decision** -- previously it wasn't centralized at all: "MuniServe: " was hand-typed into ~14 separate message-string literals across 6 files (`bplo/actions.ts`, `treasury/actions.ts`, `mayor/actions.ts`, `review-workflow.ts`, `submit-application/route.ts`, `upload-additional-document/route.ts`), so changing or conditioning it meant editing all 6 every time. New `resolveSmsIdentity(lguId, message)` in `src/lib/notifications.ts` is the one place that now decides: looks up the LGU's `sender_name`, and either sends the message as-is under that Sender Name, or prepends "MuniServe: " and sends under the account default. `notifyApplicantSms`/`notifyStaffSms` both gained a required `lguId` parameter to call it (`notifyStaffByRole` already had one, threaded straight through). Every one of the 6 call sites' message strings had their hardcoded "MuniServe: " stripped -- the prefix is applied exactly once now, not once per call site.

**Settings**: a new "SMS Notifications" card (`updateSenderName`, `settings/actions.ts`) -- separate from "Order of Payment Details" (where `treasurer_name` lives) since Sender Name affects every SMS this app sends, not just the Order of Payment. Explanatory copy reflects which state the LGU is actually in (shows the live Sender Name and confirms no prefix is added, or explains the current MuniServe-prefixed fallback) rather than static instructions, and is explicit that a Sender Name has to be purchased/approved with Semaphore directly first -- this form only records the name once that's done, it doesn't provision one.

**Verified against real production data**: confirmed San Miguel's `lgus.sender_name` is still null today, so every real applicant keeps getting the exact same prefixed SMS as before -- zero behavior change for the live pilot until an LGU actually buys a Sender Name. Confirmed via a rolled-back SQL transaction that a real BPLO session can write `sender_name` under RLS. Verified the new Settings card visually (temporary unauthenticated fixture route, same technique as this project's other staff-auth-gated UI checks, deleted immediately after) in both states -- blank/fallback copy and a set Sender Name with its own copy and pre-filled input -- confirmed via `tsc`, a full `next build`, and reading the rendered page text and input values directly, not just the source.

**Same-day follow-up: the fallback prefix itself changed from "MuniServe: " to "BPLO: ".** The project owner's own call, right after this shipped -- `resolveSmsIdentity()` in `notifications.ts` is still the single place that decides it, so this was a one-line change (plus matching comments/Settings copy) rather than touching any of the 6 call sites again, which is exactly the point of having centralized it. Since San Miguel's `sender_name` is still null in production, this is a real, immediate behavior change for the live pilot -- the very next SMS sent (an OTP code, a status update) shows "BPLO: " instead of "MuniServe: ".

---

## 7kk. Permit History: the frozen columns were silently covering the Owner column (2026-08-16)

The project owner reported the Owner column in Permit History (`businesses/history/permit-history-table.tsx`, CLAUDE.md 7f) looked cut off -- a screenshot showed names missing their first few characters ("RA M. BENJAMIN" instead of the real name, etc.), and the "Owner" column header wasn't visible at all.

**Root cause, found by reading the layout code, not by guessing at a CSS fix**: the table freezes its first three columns (Year, Permit No., Business Name) via `position: sticky`, with a hardcoded `STICKY_LEFT = [0, 96, 224]` array -- pixel offsets nobody had ever measured against those columns' actual rendered widths, which are auto-computed from content (a `<table>`'s default layout algorithm). `position: sticky` has a lesser-known behavior that made this land immediately, not just while scrolling: if an element's natural (static) position is already to the LEFT of its specified `left` offset, the browser shoves it right into compliance right away, even at rest. Since Year+Permit No.'s real combined width (short content like "2026"/"0759") was well under the guessed 224px, the sticky "Business Name" column got forced further right than its natural position -- covering part of the very next column, "Owner", which sits immediately after it in normal (non-sticky) document flow. This is why it was visible even on first load, not just when scrolling.

**Fix**: gave the three frozen columns real enforced widths (`STICKY_COL_WIDTHS = [90, 130, 220]`, applied as `width`/`minWidth`/`maxWidth` on both the header and body cells, not just a `max-width` truncation hint) and derived `STICKY_LEFT` as their running total in code, rather than as a second, independently-guessed number. With real width and offset now mathematically guaranteed to agree, there's nothing left to drift out of sync -- the class of bug (two numbers that are supposed to match but aren't derived from each other) can't recur here even if a column's content styling changes later.

**Verified directly, not just by re-reading the CSS**: built a temporary unauthenticated fixture (same technique as this project's other staff-auth-gated UI checks, deleted immediately after) rendering the real `PermitHistoryTable` component with synthetic rows matching the reported screenshot's actual data shape (short legacy-style permit numbers like "0759", a longer real `MS-2026-00001` permit number, long business/owner names). Measured the real DOM via `getBoundingClientRect()` rather than eyeballing it: confirmed the Business Name column's right edge sits exactly flush against the Owner column's left edge (0px overlap, was previously covering it) at rest, and that clicking to sort by Year or Permit No. (which appends a "▲"/"▼" to the header text) still fits inside the fixed column widths with zero clipping. Also confirmed via a full `next build` that nothing else in the table broke.

---

## 7ll. Closing the "request more info" loop (2026-08-16)

The project owner tested BPLO's own initial-review "Request more info" and found a dead end: a generic SMS ("contact the BPLO office"), no note shown to the applicant anywhere, no way to upload what was actually being asked for, and no way back in short of a phone call or a counter visit. Their proposal: SMS says to check email, email carries the actual note plus an upload link, and uploading routes the application back to whoever asked -- applied to every department, and now Treasury too (e.g. asking for a clearer copy of an attachment).

**What research turned up before building anything**: a *department's* own "request more info" already worked much better than what was tested -- the applicant's status page already showed that department's note and had an upload box right there. The real gap wasn't "nothing exists," it was that the loop was only half-closed: uploading a document never automatically got the flagged department (or BPLO) a fresh look -- someone had to separately be told, then manually click "resubmit." BPLO's own initial-review path had none of this at all, which is exactly what got tested and reported.

Two design questions were confirmed with the project owner before building (both their own recommended options):
- **Treasury's request is non-blocking** -- the applicant can still pay while it's open, matching how one department's request never halts the others (rule #5). Treasury never had any request-more-info mechanism before this pass.
- **BPLO's initial-review requeue is fully automatic** -- the moment the applicant uploads, it reopens straight in BPLO's queue and notifies them, no manual confirm step.

**One shared mechanism, not three ad hoc ones.** New `info_requests` table (migration `0041`, live in production) is the single record type behind all three reviewing surfaces (BPLO's own initial review, any department, Treasury) -- `requested_by_role` (`bplo_initial`/`department`/`treasury`), optional `department`, `notes`, who asked, and `resolved_at`. Each surface still keeps its own existing decision-recording (`department_reviews.decision`, `applications.initial_review_decision`) untouched -- `info_requests` is a parallel, purpose-built "is the applicant still expected to send us something" queue that the applicant's status page and the auto-requeue logic both read from one source, instead of each stage reconstructing that answer its own way (the applicant's status page used to query `department_reviews` directly, which only ever covered one of the three surfaces).

**`src/lib/info-requests.ts`**, three functions:
- `createInfoRequest()` -- inserts the row and notifies the applicant: SMS always (phone is guaranteed), email too when the owner has one on file (a deliberate, one-off exception to "applicants are SMS-only," same reasoning as the Order of Payment's own `notifyApplicantEmail`). The SMS says "check your email or your application status page" rather than assuming email exists -- not every applicant has one (walk-ins especially), so the status page stays the guaranteed fallback the project owner's own "check email" idea gets layered on top of, not replaces.
- `reopenDepartmentRound()` -- extracted out of `resubmitToDepartments` (the existing manual "BPLO confirms the applicant fixed it" button) so the exact same round-creation logic now powers both the manual button (kept as a fallback/escape hatch) and the new automatic path, instead of the automatic path reimplementing it a second time.
- `resolveOpenInfoRequests()` -- the actual loop-closer, called the moment an applicant's upload succeeds. Finds every open request for the application, marks them all resolved in one shot, and routes each distinct requester type back to whoever asked: a fresh department review round, BPLO's initial-review queue (flips `applications.status` back from `returned_to_applicant`), or just a Treasury notification (no status change, per the non-blocking decision). Resolves ALL open requests on one upload rather than asking the applicant which one it's for -- the upload widget has no such selector, and sending a document back to two departments when only one asked is a far cheaper mistake than the reverse.

**Every "rejected"/"request more info" decision now creates one of these**, not just the new Treasury path: `submitInitialReview` (`bplo/actions.ts`), `submitDepartmentDecision` (`review-workflow.ts` -- its own `notifyDepartmentIssue` helper is now BPLO-email-only, since `createInfoRequest` owns the applicant-facing notification), and the new `requestPaymentInfo` (`treasury/actions.ts`, same BPLO-on-behalf shape as `recordPayment`, migration 0030's precedent). Treasury's dashboard (`payment-queue.tsx`, shared with BPLO's own view) gained a collapsed-by-default "Need something from the applicant first?" toggle next to Record Payment, so it doesn't visually compete with the primary action.

**`upload-additional-document/route.ts`** now calls `resolveOpenInfoRequests()` first; if anything was actually outstanding, that already notified the right people more precisely than the old status-based "notify whoever can currently act" heuristic (which had its own latent gap -- it filtered on `decision = 'pending'`, which a department that already recorded `request_more_info` no longer matches). Only falls back to the old heuristic when nothing was actually outstanding -- the unprompted-upload case, matching the original BFP payment-proof scenario (CLAUDE.md 7c) this route was first built for.

**The applicant's status page** (`/status/[reference]/page.tsx`) gained two shared components -- `OpenInfoRequestsCard` (reads `info_requests` directly, replacing the old department-only query) and `UploadCard` (the generic "have a document to add?" prompt, now with copy that doesn't only mention BFP) -- rendered on all three now-actionable statuses: `returned_to_applicant` (previously a hardcoded dead-end message with no upload option at all), `pending_dept_review` (previously worked, now reads from the shared table), and `pending_payment` (new).

**Verified with real functional tests, not just type-checking**: three rolled-back-equivalent RLS checks confirmed BPLO can create/see every request type (including on a department's/Treasury's behalf), a department can only create and see its own, and cross-department access is correctly denied. Then a temporary, self-cleaning fixture route (same technique as this project's other staff-auth-gated verifications) created a fully synthetic test application against production, ran the real `createInfoRequest`/`resolveOpenInfoRequests` functions through all three scenarios, and confirmed: the BPLO-initial scenario correctly flipped status back to `pending_bplo_initial`; the department scenario correctly opened a fresh review round with a new `pending` row for exactly the flagged department; the Treasury scenario correctly left `applications.status` untouched (`pending_payment` before and after, confirming non-blocking); and every open request was resolved with zero left dangling. All synthetic rows (application, business, owner, notification log entries) were deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover rows in production. One real side effect worth noting: this test exercised the actual notification path against San Miguel's real Engineering department staff record, so a real (harmless, clearly test-labeled "ZZTEST-00001") email notification went to that account during verification -- not a bug, just a consequence of testing the real notify-the-right-people logic against a real LGU's real staff roster rather than a mock.

---

## 7mm. Self-service barangay list in Settings (2026-08-16)

The project owner asked directly how a new client adds their barangay list -- the honest answer at the time was "they can't, not after onboarding": `/admin`'s create-client form (CLAUDE.md 7o) was the only place that ever wrote to `lgu_form_options`' barangay rows, it only ran once at creation, and it's optional (a client can be created with none, per the project owner's own "don't block onboarding on a detail that isn't always available yet" pattern). Offered to build a real Settings section for it; the project owner said yes.

**Migration `0042_bplo_manage_barangays.sql`** (live in production): migration 0021 gave `lgu_form_options` exactly one RLS policy -- platform admins, full access, no scoping by `option_type` -- since a platform admin was the only one who'd ever touched this table. This adds a second, additive policy: BPLO can manage rows at their own LGU, but only `option_type = 'barangay'` -- the same "widen one category at a time" caution as migrations 0027/0028 (regulatory fees, then LBT/Mayor's Permit, never everything at once). `nature_of_business` is deliberately left out of this pass -- it already has a reasonable generic fallback and its own coupling to `application-form-logic.ts`'s hardcoded conditional rules that a barangay list doesn't share, so opening it up is a different, separately-scoped decision.

**`addBarangays`/`removeBarangay`** (`settings/actions.ts`): `addBarangays` accepts either a single name or a comma/newline-separated batch in the same field -- matching `/admin`'s own bulk-paste UX, useful both for a one-off fix and for a client catching up on their whole list from Settings for the first time. Silently skips any name already on file (case-insensitive) rather than erroring on the table's unique constraint, so re-pasting the same list back is a safe no-op. `removeBarangay` is a real hard delete, not the schema's usual soft-delete convention -- deliberately: nothing else references an `lgu_form_options` row by foreign key (`businesses.barangay` is a free-text value, not a pointer to this table), so there's no history to lose by actually removing one.

**Settings UI**: a new "Barangays" card, placed right before "Your public application form" (the section it most directly feeds) -- existing barangays as removable pills, an add field below, an empty-state message explicitly saying the applicant form currently shows free text instead of a dropdown when the list is empty. New `audit_log` action `barangays_updated` covers both add and remove.

**Verified against real production data**: a rolled-back transaction confirmed a real BPLO session can insert and delete a barangay row at their own LGU; a second rolled-back transaction, run as a genuine non-admin department account (the only role available for a clean negative test, since San Miguel's only "bplo" session on file is also a platform admin with separate broader access), confirmed a role outside `bplo` is correctly denied entirely. Confirmed zero leftover test rows in production afterward. The new Settings card was also visually verified (temporary unauthenticated fixture, same technique as this project's other staff-auth-gated UI checks, deleted immediately after) in both the populated and empty states.

---

## 7nn. Barangay Clearance fee (2026-08-17)

The project owner asked how Barangay Clearance is handled today. Research turned up a real, confirmed gap, not something working quietly: the applicant form has always had a "Do you have a barangay clearance?" question with exactly two options straight from the real official form (CLAUDE.md 7d) -- "Yes" (shows an upload field) and "No, generate my Brgy. clearance" -- but `fee-engine.ts` never computed anything for the "generate mine" answer. An uploaded clearance, when the applicant already had one, just landed in the same general document pile BPLO already reviews as part of the whole application (rule #9) -- no separate approval gate existed, or was asked for.

The project owner's own proposal -- a Settings choice between "Uniform Rate" and "Different Rate" (per barangay) -- was confirmed as the right shape, discussed before building per this project's standing pattern, with two open questions resolved directly (both the recommended options):
- **No separate per-document approval step.** An uploaded clearance is folded into the existing whole-application Approve/Reject/Request-more-info review, same as any other document -- if something's wrong with it specifically, BPLO uses the request-more-info loop (CLAUDE.md 7ll) already built for exactly this.
- **A missing rate blocks finalizing**, matching the existing "missing LBT category" precedent, rather than silently charging ₱0 or omitting the line.

**Deliberately no `lgus` mode column** -- same reasoning as CLAUDE.md 7ff's `cedulaIncludedOnline` (computed live from `fee_rules` rather than a second, potentially-drifting flag): a `fee_rules` row with `applies_to = '<barangay name>'` overrides the `applies_to = 'all'` uniform fallback for that one barangay, so an LGU can be "mostly uniform, two barangays are different" without an all-or-nothing switch, and there's no separate setting that could ever disagree with what's actually configured. Migration `0043` (live in production) widens `fee_rules.fee_category`'s check constraint to include `barangay_clearance` and adds the matching BPLO-scoped RLS policy, same shape as every other fee-category RLS pass (migrations 0027/0028/0038).

**`fee-engine.ts`**: a new block, only evaluated when `business.hasBarangayClearance === "No, generate my Brgy. clearance"` -- the first fee in this engine gated by a specific applicant form answer rather than an LGU setting or the business's own nature. Matches a specific-barangay row first, falling back to the uniform `"all"` row, the same "specific match, else a designated default" convention `findMayorsPermitCatalogRule` already established. A missing rate is pushed as a **warning**, not a hard `{ok:false}` block -- traced through deliberately, not by default: a hard block gets silently bypassed once Automated Assessment is off (that toggle's own existing fallback treats *any* blocked result as "fall through to manual entry"), which would let exactly the gap this feature exists to catch slip through unnoticed. Caught this by tracing the interaction, not by assuming the LBT-category precedent would transfer cleanly.

**The actual "block finalizing" behavior lives in `finalizeAssessment` instead** (`bplo/actions.ts`), as a direct, toggle-independent guard checked after `finalLines` is fully built (computed lines + manual fallback + Engineering's line, all already resolved) -- refuses to finalize when the applicant chose "generate mine" but no `barangay_clearance` line made it into the final set, regardless of whether Automated Assessment is on or off. `isManualEligible: false` throughout (a flat configured amount, same as a regulatory flat fee -- nothing a bracket lookup could get wrong, so Automated Assessment's own manual-entry fallback doesn't need to know about this category at all).

**Settings** (`setBarangayClearanceRate`, new "Barangay Clearance" card): a uniform-rate field plus one row per barangay currently on file (reusing CLAUDE.md 7mm's own barangay list) with an optional override amount and Acct Code. Clearing a per-barangay override's amount deletes that row outright, falling back to the uniform rate again; clearing the uniform row's own amount is a no-op (nothing to delete from a "not configured yet" state). Shows a pointer to the Barangays section above when the LGU hasn't added any barangays yet, rather than an empty, confusing list.

**Verified against real production data, not just type-checked**: a rolled-back transaction confirmed a real BPLO session can insert, update, and delete a `barangay_clearance` fee_rules row at their own LGU, and that a non-BPLO role is correctly denied. A temporary, self-cleaning fixture route called the real `computeApplicationFees()` directly against San Miguel's real LGU id with two real barangays (`Balaong` overridden at ₱250, the uniform rate at ₱150) and confirmed all four cases: an applicant who already has their own clearance gets no line at all; a business in the overridden barangay gets ₱250; a business in any other real barangay gets the ₱150 uniform fallback; and deleting both rules produces a warning (not a hard block) with no line pushed, matching the deliberate soft-warning design. Confirmed zero leftover fee_rules rows in production afterward. The new Settings card was also visually verified (temporary unauthenticated fixture, deleted immediately after).

**Same-day follow-up: reordered, and every Settings section made collapsible.** Two requests after seeing this shipped: "Barangays" (where the list itself is added) should come before "Barangay Clearance" (where rates are set against that list) -- moved, no logic change. And the page as a whole -- eleven sections' worth of forms and cards, all expanded at once -- was flagged as taking up too much space, not just these two specifically. New `CollapsibleSection` (`dashboard/ui.tsx`), a `<details>`/`<summary>` wrapper reusing the exact chevron-rotation mechanism the Business Registry's own expand rows already established (CLAUDE.md 7dd) -- no new CSS, no client-side state, since this page's own forms already work without JS. Every section on `/dashboard/settings` (including `StaffManagementSection`, wrapped at its call site) now uses it, collapsed by default. Verified interactively (temporary fixture, deleted after): two independent sections default closed, expanding one leaves the other closed, and the underlying `<details open>` toggling is correct -- the chevron's own rotation animation showed as unrotated in this specific automated browser environment's computed-style read for an SVG element (a plain test div with the identical class rotated correctly), which reads as a testing-environment quirk in how it resolves CSS transforms on SVG rather than a real bug, since the mechanism itself is pre-existing and unchanged from the already-shipped Business Registry usage -- flagged here rather than silently assumed fixed, worth a real-browser glance if it turns out to matter.

---

## 7oo. Archiving "Returned to applicant" (2026-08-17)

The project owner saw a real, live application ("3D-EXTREME CAR WASH") sitting in the BPLO dashboard's "Returned to applicant" section and asked whether it stays there forever, and asked the same about "Rejected". Research confirmed both halves of the concern were real, but different in kind: the "Returned to applicant" query has no date filter and nothing ever moves an application out of that status except an applicant re-submitting, so yes, it accumulates forever -- but "Rejected" turned out not to be a live problem at all, since `status = 'rejected'` is a valid CHECK-constraint value that no code path in the app ever actually sets (dead, unreachable in practice, confirmed via grep). Proposed two fixes together: collapse the section for display, and add a manual BPLO Archive/Reopen action to actually close the workflow gap. Deliberately not an auto-expiry timeout -- that would mean inventing a "how many days is too long" number with no real-world basis, which this project's standing rule is to never guess. The project owner said yes to both.

**Migration `0044_archived_application_status.sql`** (live in production): adds `'archived'` to `applications.status`'s CHECK constraint. No new RLS policy needed -- migration 0002's existing "BPLO can update applications at their own LGU" UPDATE policy already covers any status transition on a row BPLO already has access to.

**`archiveApplication`/`reopenApplication`** (`bplo/actions.ts`): BPLO-only, `returned_to_applicant` &harr; `archived` in either direction, each guarded by `.eq("status", ...)` on the expected starting state so a stale/double click is a no-op rather than a silent overwrite. Both log to `audit_log` (`application_archived`/`application_reopened`). No applicant notification on archive -- by the time BPLO archives something, they've already confirmed by phone or in person that the applicant isn't proceeding, so there's nothing new to tell them.

**`bplo/page.tsx`**: "Returned to applicant" is now wrapped in the existing `CollapsibleSection` (CLAUDE.md 7nn), collapsed by default, with an "Archive" button per row. A new "Archived" `CollapsibleSection` (only rendered when non-empty) lists archived applications with a neutral "Archived" pill and a "Reopen" button per row.

**Applicant-facing status page** (`/status/[reference]`): a new `archived` branch, matching the existing `rejected` branch's shape -- "Application closed... please visit the BPLO office" rather than the normal stage tracker.

**Business Registry fix caught proactively, not asked for**: `business-status.ts`'s `classifyBusinessStatus()` treats a business as `in_progress` (blocking it from being cleanly classified as active/needs-renewal/legacy/inactive) unless *every* application on file has a terminal status. `archived` wasn't in that `TERMINAL_STATUSES` set, which would have silently reproduced the exact same "stuck forever" bug this whole feature exists to fix, one layer up -- a business whose only application got archived would have stayed "in progress" indefinitely on the Business Registry. Added `archived` to `TERMINAL_STATUSES` alongside `released`/`rejected`. `WorkflowStepper` needed no change -- it already returns `null` for any status outside its known step list.

Also added `archived: "Archived (not proceeding)"` to the Business Registry's `APP_STATUS_LABEL` map so the status shows up readable there too, instead of falling through to a raw value.

**Verified against real production data**: a rolled-back transaction confirmed a real BPLO session can move the actual "3D-EXTREME CAR WASH" application between `returned_to_applicant` and `archived` (RLS proven, nothing persisted -- deliberately not a real write, to avoid adding test rows to this specific real client's real audit trail). A separate temporary, self-cleaning fixture route ran the real `archiveApplication`/`reopenApplication` functions end-to-end against a fully synthetic application: archive succeeded, a second archive attempt was correctly a no-op (guard held), reopen succeeded, both `audit_log` entries were written with the right `action`/`summary`, and `classifyBusinessStatus()` correctly returned non-`in_progress` while archived. All synthetic rows deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production. The new UI (both collapsible sections, the Archive/Reopen buttons, the neutral "Archived" pill) was also visually verified against mock data matching the real business name (temporary fixture, deleted immediately after).

**Same-day follow-up: manually resetting MS-2026-00006 for testing surfaced a real, pre-existing gap.** The project owner asked to put the real "3D-EXTREME CAR WASH" application back into BPLO's Initial Review queue to test the shipped changes -- done via a direct, audit-logged status update (`pending_bplo_initial`, matching where its own `audit_log` trail showed it had been returned FROM) since no UI action exists for "undo a return." Testing the resulting "request more info" email from a different browser than the one MS-2026-00006 was originally filed from reproduced a real, already-documented limitation: `/status/[reference]`'s "Can't verify this application here" branch, which exists because the applicant session is an `httpOnly` cookie set only in the browser that submitted (`applicant-session.ts`) -- a comment on that branch, written when the info_requests feature (CLAUDE.md 7ll) shipped, already flagged a phone-based re-verification flow as "reasonable future work." The project owner asked to build it now.

**New `send-status-otp`/`route.ts` + a new `applicationId` branch on the existing shared `verify-otp`/`route.ts`**: same shape as the renewal flow's own `businessId` branch (CLAUDE.md, Permit Number renewal, 2026-08-16) -- the phone is resolved server-side from the application's own business/owner, never accepted from the client, and only ever shown to the client as a masked hint (`maskPhone`). Since an application always implies an already-claimed business, this hits the exact same `existingOwner` branch every other `verify-otp` path already uses -- no new identity-resolution logic, just a new way to resolve which phone to check.

**New `VerifyPhoneCard`** (`status/[reference]/verify-phone-form.tsx`): renders inside the existing "Can't verify" card whenever the business has a phone on file -- send code, enter code, verify, reload. On success the normal applicant_session cookie gets set for that browser too, same as any other sign-in path, so the rest of the page (and any future visit from that browser) just works.

**Verified against real functionality, deliberately without spending a real Semaphore SMS credit on a fake recipient**: a fully synthetic owner/business/application (fake phone, `ZZTEST-VERIFYPHONE-0001`) confirmed the new nested-select join in `send-status-otp` resolves the right phone (temporary fixture route, deleted after), and a direct call to `verify-otp` with a manually-inserted OTP code (bypassing Semaphore entirely, since `sendOtpCode` itself is unchanged, shared, already-proven code) confirmed: a wrong code is correctly rejected (400), a correct code sets the session cookie, and reloading `/status/ZZTEST-VERIFYPHONE-0001` afterward showed the normal stage tracker instead of "Can't verify" -- proving the cookie actually took effect end-to-end, not just that the API returned 200. `send-status-otp`'s own error paths (missing/bogus `applicationId`) were confirmed to fail before ever reaching Semaphore. All synthetic rows (owner, business, application, otp_codes, applicant_sessions) deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production.

## 7pp. Document uploads bypass Vercel's function body limit entirely (2026-08-17)

Testing the phone re-verification feature above, the project owner hit "Couldn't upload a file, Try PDF or image under 10MB" trying to attach a scanned document, and flagged 10MB as too small for a real scan. The actual cause turned out to be a different, worse number than the one in the error message: tested directly against production with files of increasing size, a 4MB upload reached our code fine, but everything at 4.5MB and up came back `413` from **Vercel's platform itself**, before our route ever ran. Vercel's own serverless function request-body ceiling sits between 4MB and 4.5MB -- our declared "10MB" check was already dead code for anything above that; nothing between roughly 4.3MB and 10MB could ever have succeeded, regardless of what `MAX_FILE_BYTES` said. Raising that number further would have changed nothing, since the real ceiling isn't something the app controls. Presented this to the project owner as two options -- fix the declared number to be honest (quick, doesn't solve large scans) vs. actually rearchitect uploads to route around the platform limit (real fix, more work) -- and they chose the real fix.

**New architecture: the browser uploads directly to Supabase Storage, never through a Vercel function.** `request-upload-url/route.ts` (new) does the only thing that still needs server auth -- verifies the applicant session, then asks Storage for a signed upload URL scoped to a path prefixed with the caller's own `ownerId` (the same ownership convention `upload-document.ts` already used for pre-submission orphaned documents). The browser then PUTs the file straight to Supabase via that signed URL (`supabase.storage....uploadToSignedUrl`) -- Vercel's request-body limit never applies, since the file bytes never pass through any of our functions. `upload-document.ts` and `upload-additional-document.ts` both changed from "receive the file, store it" to "the browser says it uploaded something at this path -- register it": each now re-verifies the object actually exists at that path and its real stored size (`verifyUploadedObject`, new shared helper in `lib/document-upload.ts`), deleting and rejecting anything that's somehow oversized rather than trusting the client's claim.

**Three places now agree on the same 25MB ceiling**, each independently authoritative for a different reason: the bucket's own `file_size_limit` (`storage.buckets`, set directly via SQL -- Storage's own enforced limit, unspoofable by the client, the real backstop), `verifyUploadedObject`'s post-upload re-check (catches anything that somehow got past the bucket limit), and the client-side pre-check in both `ApplyPageClient.tsx` and `AdditionalDocumentUpload` (immediate feedback before even attempting an upload, not authoritative on its own -- a client could lie about `file.size`, but the two server-side checks don't depend on it). Content-type validation didn't get any stricter than before: `request-upload-url` still only checks the client-declared `contentType` against the same allow-list `upload-document.ts` always checked, since that was always just trusting a client-reported MIME type either way -- this change doesn't weaken that, it was never deeper than that to begin with.

**Verified against real production Storage, not just logic**: a synthetic application (`ZZTEST-UPLOAD-0001`) confirmed the full path end-to-end -- requested a real signed URL through the actual route, PUT a real 20MB file directly to Supabase (200, bypassing Vercel entirely), registered it through `upload-additional-document`, and confirmed the `documents` row and the object's real stored size (20,971,520 bytes, exact) both landed correctly. A 30MB file was correctly rejected by the bucket's own `file_size_limit` with a clean `EntityTooLarge` error before ever becoming a document row. Also confirmed: an unsupported content type is rejected at the `request-upload-url` step, and a path outside the caller's own `ownerId` prefix is rejected at the register step. All synthetic rows and the uploaded storage object were deleted afterward (the storage object via a temporary fixture route, since direct SQL deletes on `storage.objects` are blocked by Supabase's own protective trigger) -- confirmed zero leftover data in production.

**Same-day follow-up: shipped, then immediately broke for a completely different reason.** The project owner retested against the real "3D-EXTREME CAR WASH" application right after this deployed and got a new, generic "Could not save that upload. Try again." on every attempt -- different files, different types, always the same result. Checked runtime logs first rather than guessing: `request-upload-url` was succeeding (200) and the file really was landing in Storage at the right size every time, but the final register call to `upload-additional-document` came back 400 every time. That narrowed it to one specific check.

The actual cause was two pre-existing gaps stacked together, neither introduced by this feature: `UploadCard`'s `AdditionalDocumentUpload` has always been called with `defaultLabel=""` (`status/[reference]/page.tsx`), and `documentType` has always been a required field server-side -- so anyone who chose a file without first typing something into the "What is this document?" box (no visual cue that it's required) got a real, silent `invalid_request` 400, just with a much less specific error message before this feature shipped (the old error also just said "try a PDF or image under 10MB" regardless of the real reason). This was always reachable; today's testing is just what surfaced it.

Fixed two ways: `UploadCard` now pre-fills `defaultLabel` from the most recent open `info_requests` note for that application (e.g. "Please submit a clearer copy of DTI.") instead of leaving it blank -- since BPLO already said what they want, there's no reason to make the applicant re-type it, and this alone fixes the common case outright. `AdditionalDocumentUpload`'s `handleFile` also gained a client-side guard rejecting an empty `documentType` before attempting any network call at all, with an accurate message ("Please describe what this document is before uploading") -- so this specific failure mode can never again surface as a generic, unexplained error regardless of what the default is.

While building the fix, a second real bug surfaced the same way (caught by a query erroring out, not by guessing): the first attempt at the `info_requests` pre-fill query ordered by a `created_at` column that doesn't exist on that table (it's `requested_at` -- confirmed via `information_schema.columns` rather than assumed) that returns `null` on error, one of the reasons every verification in this project runs the actual query end-to-end rather than trusting that similarly-shaped code will behave the same way.

**Verified against real functionality**: a synthetic application with an open `info_requests` row (`ZZTEST-DOCFIX-0001`, `notes = "Please submit a clearer copy of DTI."`) confirmed the field now pre-fills with that exact text (read directly from the rendered DOM, not just the API response), and a full upload through the real pre-filled value succeeded end-to-end (signed URL, direct PUT, register, `{ok:true}`). All synthetic rows -- including `audit_log`/`notifications_log` entries the real `resolveOpenInfoRequests` notification path wrote as a side effect of this being a genuinely real round-trip -- were deleted afterward; confirmed zero leftover data in production.

---

## 7qq. Full pipeline audit, then the top 3 findings fixed (2026-08-17)

The project owner asked for a full lifecycle review -- every role's journey (applicant, BPLO, department, treasury, mayor), every decision branch (approved, approved with condition, request more info, rejected), explicitly framed as "put yourself as an applicant, as BPLO staff... check for gaps, loopholes, second order consequences." Ran four parallel research passes tracing the actual current code (department review, BPLO/Treasury/Mayor pipeline, applicant/OTP/upload journey, systemic RLS + renewal/lifecycle gaps), cross-checked against real production data, and published a structured findings report (23 items: 3 high, 13 medium, 7 low) rather than just listing them in chat. The project owner said to go ahead and fix the top three.

**1. OTP verification had no brute-force protection.** `sendOtpCode`'s 30-second cooldown only throttled how fast a *new* code could be requested -- nothing counted or limited failed *verify* attempts against an already-sent code. A 6-digit code, live for 5 minutes, with zero attempt limiting is realistically brute-forceable by a scripted loop, and this is the one credential the entire app runs on (login, renewal, the status-page re-verification built earlier today). Fixed with migration `0045_otp_attempts.sql` (`otp_codes.attempts`, live in production) and a rewrite of `verifyOtp()` (`src/lib/otp.ts`): now looks up the single latest active row for a phone (not "whichever row happens to match the submitted code," which also means only the most-recently-sent code is ever checkable -- a correct side effect, not just infrastructure for counting), locks a code out after 5 wrong guesses regardless of what's typed afterward, and increments `attempts` on every miss. A legitimate user just requests a fresh code (which the existing "Resend" button already does) -- a new row starts at `attempts = 0`.

**2. `signPermit` did the irreversible parts before checking the application was ready to sign.** The application fetch had no `status` filter at all -- the `permits` row insert, the `permit_history` append, generating and publishing the permit PDF to a *public* bucket, and the "ready for pickup" SMS all happened unconditionally, with the actual `pending_mayor` status check arriving dead last, as the final `applications` UPDATE. It was only ever saved from issuing a real permit on an application that hadn't reached signing by `permits.permit_number`'s own unique-constraint collision on a *re*-sign -- an application still mid-department-review, say, had nothing stopping it from sailing straight through. Fixed (`src/app/dashboard/mayor/actions.ts`) by adding `.eq("status", "pending_mayor")` to the initial guarded fetch itself, matching the same guard-first pattern `markPrinted`/`markReleased` (`bplo/actions.ts`) already used correctly -- now the very first thing this function does is confirm the application is actually at the right stage, before any of its real side effects run.

**3. "Reject" and "Request info" were status-identical at BPLO's initial review.** Both decisions just sent the application to `returned_to_applicant`, which loops back for resubmission either way -- there was no single action that permanently closed an application at this stage, despite Reject's label clearly implying otherwise. The only real way to end something was Reject, then separately find it and click the manual Archive action built earlier today. Fixed in `submitInitialReview` (`bplo/actions.ts`): a `"rejected"` decision now goes straight to `archived` -- the same terminal status and the same "Application closed" applicant messaging the manual Archive action already established, reusing existing infrastructure rather than inventing a second terminal state. No `info_requests` row gets created for this branch (there's nothing left to ask for), replaced with a direct rejection notification. New `AuditAction` value `initial_review_rejected`, distinct from a later manual archive, so the trail shows *how* an application actually closed. `reopenApplication` (already built) needed no change -- `archived` is `archived` regardless of how it got there, so BPLO can still walk this back the same way as any manually-archived application.

**Verified against real functionality, not just logic**: OTP lockout confirmed end-to-end against a synthetic phone -- 5 wrong guesses each correctly rejected, a 6th attempt with the *correct* code still rejected once locked out, `attempts` capped at exactly 5 in the database, and a freshly-sent code (a new row, `attempts = 0`) verified normally. `signPermit`'s guard confirmed both ways against a synthetic application: the exact guarded-fetch query correctly returned zero rows while the application sat at `pending_dept_review`, then correctly returned the row once flipped to `pending_mayor`. The reject-to-archive path confirmed via a fixture exercising the exact same branch logic `submitInitialReview` now runs: status landed on `archived`, zero `info_requests` rows were created, and the audit log carried the new `initial_review_rejected` action with the right summary. All synthetic rows across all three tests deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production.

**Same-day follow-up: the project owner asked to keep going through the rest of the report.** Three more findings closed in this pass:

**Fee-override validation + a real double-submit race in `finalizeAssessment`.** An override used to accept literally anything typed into it -- a stray letter silently became `NaN` and poisoned the total, and nothing stopped a negative amount either. `parseOverrideAmount()` now rejects anything that isn't a valid, non-negative number, with no invented upper ceiling (this project's own standing rule against guessing a real-world limit). Separately: the `application_fee_lines` insert used to happen *before* the guarded status transition, so two near-simultaneous finalize calls (a double-click before the button disables, two tabs) could both pass the initial fetch's guard and both insert a full duplicate set of fee lines -- the loser's status update then silently affected zero rows with no error surfaced at all. Fixed by claiming the status transition FIRST (a single guarded `.update().select().single()`, same shape as `signPermit`'s own fix above) -- Postgres serializes concurrent updates against the same row, so only one caller's claim can ever succeed, and the loser throws immediately, before ever touching fee lines. If the fee-lines insert itself then fails, the claim is compensated (rolled back to `pending_bplo_assessment`) rather than leaving the application at `pending_payment` with nothing recorded -- the same "undo what already succeeded" shape this project's fee-rule CSV import (7s) already established, since there's no real multi-statement transaction available here.

**`recordPayment` had no `applications.status` precondition on the `payments` INSERT itself.** The insert happened first, with the status guard arriving only afterward -- a stale page or fast double-click could create a real `payments` row against an application that wasn't actually `pending_payment`, left behind with nothing cleaning it up once the later guard failed. Fixed with the identical claim-first shape: the status transition is claimed before the payment is ever inserted, with a compensating rollback if the insert fails.

**Archive widened to close any stuck application, not just "Returned to applicant."** The manual Archive action built earlier today only ever worked from `returned_to_applicant` -- an application stalled mid-department-review, half-assessed, waiting on a payment that never comes, or sitting at printing/signing for months had no equivalent, so it just sat there forever. New `applications.archived_from_status` (migration `0046`) remembers which stage an application was actually archived from, so `reopenApplication` restores the real prior stage instead of always assuming `returned_to_applicant` -- reads the current status first, then a guarded update re-checks that same status (a status change racing in between fails safely rather than archiving from a stage BPLO never saw). Deliberately excludes `pending_release`: the Mayor has already signed by that point (CLAUDE.md 7i -- the permit is legally issued there, release is just the physical hand-off), so "archived" would misleadingly read as "no permit" when one genuinely exists. Archive buttons added to every other queue on `bplo/page.tsx` (departments-review card, assessment card, printing, at-the-Mayor's-office) and to the shared `AwaitingPaymentSection` (a new `showArchive` prop, BPLO's own render passes it, Treasury's never does -- closing an application out isn't Treasury's call). The Archived section now shows which stage each application was closed from.

**Verified against real production data**: the widened archive/reopen logic confirmed end-to-end against a synthetic application -- archiving from `pending_payment` correctly stored `archived_from_status: "pending_payment"` and reopening correctly restored it (not the old hardcoded `returned_to_applicant`); archiving from `pending_release` was correctly refused. `parseOverrideAmount` confirmed against every real input shape (garbage, negative, zero, decimal, `Infinity`/`NaN` strings) -- only genuinely invalid input rejected. `recordPayment`'s claim-first guard confirmed directly: a wrong-status application's claim failed exactly as `signPermit`'s own guard already proved, and critically, zero `payments` rows were ever created in that failure case -- the phantom-row problem this fix exists to close. All synthetic rows deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production.

**Same-day follow-up: three structural dead-ends in department review, closed.** `areAllDepartmentsCleared` used to re-derive "who must decide" from a LIVE query of `lgu_departments.is_active` at check-time, rather than the `department_reviews` rows actually created when a round opened -- two real dead-ends followed. A department deactivated after a round opened had its still-pending row silently dropped from the check (the round "cleared" without it ever deciding, no trace anything was skipped). A department activated after a round opened was required by the live check despite having no row in that round at all -- and nothing anywhere ever creates one for an already-open round, so that application could never clear again, full stop. Fixed by basing "cleared" on the department_reviews rows the function has actually seen (`latestByDept`'s own keys), not a live re-query -- a department with no row for this application was never asked and isn't required; one that DID get a row keeps being required for real even after deactivation, visibly stuck rather than silently skipped, recoverable via the widened Archive action above if it's truly a dead end. Dropped `areAllDepartmentsCleared`'s now-unused `lguId` parameter (its one caller updated to match).

A third, related dead-end: an LGU with zero active departments left `openDepartmentReviewRound`'s own `review_rounds` row with zero `department_reviews` children -- and since nothing but a real department decision ever calls `areAllDepartmentsCleared`, with no departments left to decide anything, nothing would ever trigger that check. The application sat at `pending_dept_review` forever with no path forward at all. `openDepartmentReviewRound` now detects this directly and advances the application straight to `pending_bplo_assessment` itself, the same place a normal round lands once every department actually clears -- there's nothing to wait for if there's no one left to ask.

**Verified against the real functions, not a replica**: `review-workflow.ts` has no `"use server"` directive, so a temporary fixture (deleted after) called `openDepartmentReviewRound`/`areAllDepartmentsCleared` directly -- no logic re-implemented for the test. Built a synthetic LGU with two departments: opening a round created exactly 2 pending rows; approving one and deactivating the other confirmed `cleared` stayed correctly `false` (the old code would have wrongly reported `true` here); adding a third, never-involved department mid-round and then resolving the original two confirmed `cleared` correctly flipped to `true`, unblocked by the new department. A separate synthetic application with all departments deactivated confirmed `openDepartmentReviewRound` created zero rows and immediately advanced straight to `pending_bplo_assessment`. All synthetic rows (LGU, departments, owner, businesses, applications, rounds, reviews) deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production. Also confirmed the real, live San Miguel application actively in department review during this work reached `released` by the end of it, undisturbed by any of these changes.

**Same-day follow-up: three more findings.**

**"Approved with condition"'s own condition text used to vanish the moment department review opened** -- visible nowhere in the normal review/assessment flow, only as an unstructured JSON blob in the audit log. Now surfaced everywhere the application appears while the condition is still relevant: BPLO's "In review across departments" card, `AssessmentCard` (both the normal and the blocked-result early-return path), and the department's own review card (`department/page.tsx`) -- so a department reviewing next actually sees why BPLO only conditionally approved it, not just that they did. Reused the `cond` tone token (`bg-cond-bg`/`text-cond-ink`) `TonePill` already established, rather than inventing a new visual treatment. Each department's own notes (approved-with-condition, rejected, or request-more-info) are now shown next to their pill on BPLO's dept-review card too -- previously only the decision label rendered, never the reasoning behind it, for any decision type.

**Treasury's own "request more info" could quietly outlive its own relevance.** Deliberately non-blocking by design (`applications.status` never moves for it), but nothing ever resolved it once the application advanced past `pending_payment` -- the applicant's status page stops showing the info-request UI for any later status, so a still-open Treasury request just sat there forever with no path to close it. `recordPayment` now auto-resolves any of Treasury's own still-open requests the moment payment is actually recorded -- Treasury choosing to proceed with recording payment is itself the explicit call to move on regardless of what they'd asked for. Deliberately scoped to `requested_by_role = "treasury"` only, so a department's or BPLO-initial's request (which shouldn't coexist with `pending_payment` anyway) is never touched.

**The "currently stuck" table on Performance Stats had two real blind spots.** `returned_to_applicant` was missing from its own status-label map entirely, so it never appeared in the stuck list at all -- the exact status the manual Archive action exists to close out, invisible on the one dashboard meant to surface exactly this. Added it (`STATUS_LABEL`), using `initial_review_at` as the "entered this stage" timestamp. Separately, and more seriously: the stuck-applications computation used to reuse the same date-range-filtered `applications` array as every other (genuinely historical) stat on that page -- so an application open longer than the selected window (90 days by default) silently fell out of view entirely, for exactly the worst case, since the longest-stuck applications are the ones most likely to predate the window. Split into its own `computeStuckApplications()`, an entirely separate, unbounded query run regardless of the page's selected range (and regardless of whether the range itself has zero applications in it, which used to short-circuit the whole function before this was split out) -- "stuck" is inherently a live-right-now question, not a historical-range one.

**Verified against real production data**: a synthetic application confirmed the info_requests auto-resolve fires correctly on payment recording (`resolved_at` set). A second synthetic pair -- one at `returned_to_applicant`, one backdated 150 days into the past at `pending_payment` -- confirmed both now appear in `computeStuckApplications()`'s output even when queried against a 7-day range that structurally excludes both from the historical stats entirely; the 150-day one's `daysStuck` came back as exactly ~150, confirming the unbounded fetch. All synthetic rows deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production.

**Same-day follow-up: document uploads had no cleanup mechanism at all, and one kind of leftover was already real.** Confirmed 12 orphaned `documents` rows already existed in production at audit time -- abandoned pre-submission uploads (`upload-document.ts`, `application_id` still null) whose form was never finished. A second, worse kind is invisible to any DB query entirely: a real Storage object whose PUT succeeded but whose follow-up "register" call (`upload-document.ts`/`upload-additional-document.ts`) never happened at all, for any reason (dropped connection, closed tab) -- nothing but a real Storage listing can find one.

New daily cron (`/api/cron/cleanup-orphaned-uploads`, `vercel.json`, same `CRON_SECRET` auth as `department-reminders`): deletes orphaned `documents` rows (and their Storage object) after 7 days, and separately enumerates every owner folder in the bucket, diffing against every known `documents.file_url` to find real Storage objects with no row at all, deleting anything unmatched older than 24 hours -- a grace window well past how long a register call could ever legitimately take after a successful upload, so it can't race a genuinely in-flight upload. Both thresholds are a technical/operational judgment call, not a domain fact requiring confirmation (unlike a fee rate or legal deadline) -- generous on both counts, adjustable if it ever needs tuning.

Also closed the other half of the same finding: nothing previously limited how many times one authenticated owner could call `request-upload-url` -- each call reserves a fresh 25MB-capped slot, so a scripted burst could accumulate unbounded real Storage usage with zero app-level throttle. Now caps at 50 existing objects per owner (a real application only ever needs a handful) before refusing new slots (`429 too_many_uploads`).

**Verified against real functionality, not just logic**: built four synthetic cases in Storage/`documents` directly -- a Storage object with no row, backdated 48 hours; an identical one left fresh; a `documents` row 10 days old with a real Storage object behind it; an identical one only 1 day old. Ran the actual cron route (real `CRON_SECRET` auth, not a bypass) and confirmed exactly the right pair got deleted -- the backdated Storage object and the 10-day-old row (plus its file) -- while both fresh ones survived untouched. The rate limit confirmed separately: a real signed-URL request succeeded normally below the cap, then failed with `429` immediately after bulk-creating 50 objects under that same owner's folder. All synthetic Storage objects, `documents` rows, and the synthetic owner/session deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production. The 12 real orphaned rows already in production were deliberately left for the actual scheduled cron to clean up naturally after deployment, rather than manually triggered against production during verification.

**Same-day follow-up: Pause (CLAUDE.md 7o) didn't actually stop an already-open staff session.** Confirmed by tracing every enforcement point: pause only ever blocked a fresh page load (`dashboard/layout.tsx`'s own render-time check) and new applicant submissions (`submit-application/route.ts`) -- neither RLS nor a single one of the ~37 state-changing server actions across BPLO, Treasury, Mayor, department, and Settings ever looked at `is_paused` at all. A staff member with a tab already open from before the pause could keep approving, recording payments, signing permits, or managing settings indefinitely through it.

Fixed with a new `requireUnpausedStaff()` (`src/lib/staff.ts`), deliberately **not** folded into `getCurrentStaff()` itself -- that function is also what `dashboard/layout.tsx` calls to decide whether to render `PausedNotice` in the first place; if it silently returned `null` for a paused real staff member, the layout's own pause-detection branch would never run, and a paused visitor would fall through to whatever a page's own `getCurrentStaff()` call does next (usually a redirect to `/login`) instead of ever seeing the actual "Your account is currently Paused" message -- a real regression, not a fix. `requireUnpausedStaff()` wraps `getCurrentStaff()` and adds the `is_paused` check on top, exempting a platform admin's "view as" proxy row exactly like the layout's own existing exemption (they still need to act on a paused client's behalf while troubleshooting it).

Every actual state-changing action already called `getCurrentStaff()` as its first line -- a mechanical one-line swap (`const staff = await requireUnpausedStaff();`) across all 8 action files (`bplo`, `treasury`, `mayor`, `department`, `businesses`, and three `settings` action files) closes the gap everywhere at once, with the exact same `if (!staff || staff.role !== "...")` guard immediately after already handling the paused (now-`null`) case correctly, unchanged. Confirmed every one of the 37 call sites across those 8 files matched the identical literal pattern before doing the swap (call-site count plus one import line accounted for the total occurrences in each file, verified per file) -- no call site was special-cased or missed.

**Verified against real functionality**: since `requireUnpausedStaff()` needs a real Google OAuth session that can't be driven in this environment, isolated and tested the actual new branching logic directly (the part after `getCurrentStaff()` resolves a row -- `getCurrentStaff()`'s own auth chain is unchanged and already proven extensively elsewhere this session) against a synthetic paused LGU: a real-staff-shaped check correctly blocked; an admin-proxy-shaped check on the same paused LGU correctly did not; a real-staff-shaped check against San Miguel's own real, unpaused LGU correctly did not block either. Confirmed `dashboard/layout.tsx`'s own pause-display logic is provably untouched -- `getCurrentStaff()` itself was never modified, only a new function added alongside it. Synthetic LGU deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production.

**Same-day follow-up: BPLO could act on a department's behalf using a stale, superseded round's review row, and Treasury/Mayor had no read access to `department_reviews` at all.**

`submitDepartmentDecisionAsBplo`/`submitDepartmentDecision` (`review-workflow.ts`) took `departmentReviewId` straight from form data with no check that the row belonged to the CURRENT/latest review round for its application -- the UI (`bplo/page.tsx`) only ever renders an "Act for {department}" button for a pending row in the latest round, but RLS only enforces "same LGU," not "this is the round that's actually open right now," so a crafted or stale form submission could reference an older, superseded round's still-pending row and still succeed server-side. Fixed by fetching the row's own round + the application's actual latest round number up front, before any write, throwing "This review is from an earlier round and is no longer open" if they disagree -- the same "guard before touching data" shape this function already used for the Engineering amount check right below it. Applies equally to a department's own dashboard action and BPLO's on-behalf action, since both call this one shared function.

Separately: `department_reviews` has only ever had SELECT/write policies for `bplo` (full access) and `department` (own-department scoped) roles (migration 0002) -- Treasury and Mayor logins had no policy on this table at all, so an embedded `department_reviews(...)` join from either of their own RLS-scoped sessions would silently return null rows instead of erroring, the identical shape to two real bugs already hit in this project (`owners` had no policy until migration 0023; `current_staff()` picked the wrong row until migration 0029). Nothing reads it that way today -- closed anyway rather than waiting for a future feature to hit it. New additive SELECT-only policy, migration `0047_department_reviews_treasury_mayor_select.sql` (live in production), scoped by LGU the same join-shape as the existing `bplo` policy.

**Verified against real production data**: the round-scoping guard's exact query shape (department_reviews -> its round's round_number, vs. the application's max round_number) was run directly against a synthetic two-round application built at San Miguel -- a department_reviews row from round 1 correctly evaluated as failing the guard (`would_pass_guard: false`) once round 2 existed, while round 2's own row correctly passed (`true`); `submitDepartmentDecision` itself has no `"use server"` directive but does construct its own cookie-session client internally, so the guard's underlying data relationships were verified directly rather than through a driven session, matching this project's established fallback technique for session-gated logic. The new RLS policy was verified two ways: a real BPLO/department staff session at San Miguel, temporarily flipped to `treasury` and separately to `mayor` inside connection-scoped (not explicitly committed) SQL, correctly saw all 30 real `department_reviews` rows at that LGU where it previously saw zero; both role flips were confirmed to not persist afterward. All synthetic rows (owner, business, application, 2 rounds, 2 department_reviews) deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production.

**Same-day follow-up: applicants had no way to actually sign out.** `ApplyPageClient`'s "Start over" button only ever reset local React state -- the real `applicant_session` cookie (and its server-side `applicant_sessions` row, valid 30 days) stayed live regardless of what the UI showed, and `/status/[reference]`'s authenticated view had no sign-out control anywhere. On a shared/public device (a barangay hall terminal, a borrowed phone to get OTP-verified quickly), the next person on that browser stayed silently signed in as the previous applicant for up to 30 days -- able to submit a brand-new application under the wrong owner_id with no OTP required again (`submit-application/route.ts` trusts the cookie's `ownerId` outright), or view that person's own application status pages.

New `signOutApplicant()` in a dedicated `src/lib/applicant-session-actions.ts` -- deliberately its own file with its own top-level `"use server"` directive, not added to `applicant-session.ts` itself: that file also exports a plain synchronous helper (`applicantSessionCookieOptions`) and is imported by ordinary API routes, so it can't carry the file-level directive Next.js requires before a Client Component can import and call a Server Function directly (confirmed against this project's own installed Next 16 docs before writing it, per AGENTS.md's standing warning not to trust training data for this specific version -- a function-level inline `"use server"` only works for actions defined directly inside a Server Component's own render function, not a plain lib file imported by a Client Component). A real revoke, not just a cookie clear: deletes the matching `applicant_sessions` row via service-role before clearing the cookie, so the token itself stops working rather than merely stopping being sent.

Wired into both real gaps: `ApplyPageClient`'s `startOver()` now actually calls it before resetting local state, and a new `SignOutButton` client component ("Not you? Sign out") appears on every authenticated view of `/status/[reference]` (the normal stage tracker, and the `returned_to_applicant`/`rejected`/`archived` branches) -- the one surface that never had any sign-out affordance at all.

**Verified against real production data, not just logic**: a temporary, self-cleaning fixture route (`/api/test-signout-fixture`, deleted immediately after) created a real synthetic `owners` row and `applicant_sessions` row with a known token, set that token as the request's own cookie, then called the real `signOutApplicant()` and confirmed the session row count went from 1 to 0. Separately confirmed via the actual browser (`document.cookie`, read after the request completed) that the real outgoing response genuinely cleared the cookie client-side, even though the same in-request `cookies().get()` readback didn't reflect the deletion immediately -- a documented quirk of Next's mutable cookie store, not a bug, and not something that affects the real browser's own behavior, which is what actually matters. All synthetic rows deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover owners or sessions in production.

**Same-day follow-up: `verify-otp` accepted ambiguous request shapes, and its `legacyBusinessId` claim step never re-checked which LGU the business actually belonged to.** Three separate mechanisms decide whose phone an OTP is checked against -- a client-typed `phone`, a `businessId` (renewal-by-Permit-Number), or an `applicationId` (status-page re-verification) -- but nothing required exactly one of them; sending more than one silently let whichever branch happened to run last (`applicationId`, then `businessId`) win with no error, a request shape that should never occur in normal use. Now rejected outright (400) unless exactly one is present, `code` is exactly 6 digits (the only shape `otp.ts` ever actually generates), and `legacyBusinessId` is never combined with `businessId`/`applicationId` -- semantically invalid, since claiming a fresh unclaimed business only ever makes sense on the plain-phone first-claim screen.

Separately: `lookup-license/route.ts` (the only normal way a client ever learns a `legacyBusinessId`) already scopes its search to the request's own resolved LGU -- but `verify-otp`'s own claim step never re-checked that, trusting a different route's scoping had already happened. A crafted request could otherwise pass any still-unclaimed `legacyBusinessId` regardless of which LGU it actually belongs to (low real-world exploitability -- UUIDs aren't guessable -- but the same "don't trust a different route's own scoping" principle this project's RLS policies already follow everywhere else). Now re-checks `businesses.lgu_id` against `resolveLguId(host)` before claiming.

**Verified against real functionality, not just logic**: with a real second live client on file (Cavite, alongside San Miguel), built two synthetic still-unclaimed legacy businesses -- one at each LGU -- plus a real `otp_codes` row (bypassing Semaphore, matching this project's established technique). Drove the actual running route via real `fetch()` calls from the browser: a 2-digit code, a request with both `phone` and `businessId`, and a request combining `businessId` with `legacyBusinessId` were all correctly rejected with 400 before ever reaching `verifyOtp()`. A claim attempt against the Cavite business from a `localhost` request (which resolves to San Miguel, the pilot fallback) correctly failed with `legacy_business_not_found`, and the Cavite row was confirmed still `is_legacy_unclaimed: true` with no owner afterward -- the cross-tenant claim genuinely didn't take effect, not just returned the right error code. The identical request against the matching same-LGU business succeeded (200), created a real owner pre-filled with the legacy name, and the business's `is_legacy_unclaimed` flipped to `false` with the new `owner_id` set -- confirming the legitimate path still works unchanged. All synthetic rows (2 businesses, 1 owner, 1 otp_codes row, 1 applicant_sessions row the successful call created) deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production.

**Same-day follow-up: no way to regenerate a signed permit's PDF once generation failed at signing.** `signPermit`'s own PDF/QR generation is deliberately best-effort (CLAUDE.md 7k -- a renderer/upload bug must never undo or block a real signature), which means `permits.pdf_url`/`qr_code_url` can stay null forever with nothing anywhere to retry it. Before this, that failure was permanent: the applicant's `/status` page fell back to "pick up at the BPLO counter" for good, with no path to ever offer a download, and there was also no way to re-render a corrected file after fixing an LGU display-text typo that had already been baked into a previously-generated PDF.

New `regeneratePermitPdf` (`businesses/actions.ts`), BPLO-only -- reuses the exact same `generatePermitAssets()` call and storage paths `signPermit` already uses (`upsert: true` overwrites the existing object at the same path, so this is a true re-render, not a second copy or a new URL to reconcile). Deliberately doesn't touch `permit_history`, `issued_at`, or `valid_until` -- nothing about the real permit changes, only the artifact. The final `permits` UPDATE uses the service-role client, matching `signPermit`'s own choice there -- `permits` has INSERT policies (mayor, migration 0002; BPLO-on-behalf, migration 0032) but no staff-scoped UPDATE policy at all. New `permit_pdf_regenerated` audit action. Surfaced as a "Regenerate permit PDF" button on the Business Registry's per-business application history, next to the existing "Submitted form (PDF)"/"Order of Payment" links, shown once an application has reached `pending_release` or `released` (the two statuses a `permits` row can exist at).

**Verified against real production Storage and data, not just logic**: built a fully synthetic signed-permit scenario (owner, business, application at `released`, a `permits` row with real `issued_at`/`valid_until` and `pdf_url`/`qr_code_url` both still null -- exactly the "generation failed at signing" state this feature exists to fix). A temporary, self-cleaning fixture route (deleted immediately after) replicated the action's exact query shape and generation call via service-role -- the only piece that can't be driven without a real Google-OAuth staff session, matching this project's established fallback for every other gated dashboard action -- and confirmed: a real, valid PDF (`%PDF-` magic bytes, 7,253 bytes) and QR PNG (2,747 bytes) were generated and uploaded, the `permits` row was re-read from the database afterward and genuinely carried the new public URLs (not just a claimed-successful write), and the "hasn't been signed yet" guard correctly returns nothing for an application with no `permits` row. All synthetic rows and both real Storage objects (the Storage object delete needed the Storage API's own `.remove()`, not direct SQL, per this schema's `protect_objects_delete` trigger) deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production.

**Same-day follow-up, closing the last finding: staff-assisted business un-claim/reassign.** `updateOwnerPhone` (CLAUDE.md, 2026-08-16) only ever changes the phone on the SAME owner already linked to a business -- there was no way to fix a business linked to the outright wrong person (wrong owner picked at the counter, someone claiming a business that wasn't actually theirs), or to detach it and let it be claimed again.

New `unclaimBusiness` (`businesses/actions.ts`), BPLO-only. Deliberately reuses `is_legacy_unclaimed = true` as the "no owner, available to be (re)claimed" state rather than adding a new column or a separate un-claim flag -- that's already the one thing this flag means everywhere else in the schema (`business-status.ts`), and setting it is what makes the *existing* `claimLegacyBusiness` form (gated on exactly this flag) immediately usable again on the same row with no changes to it at all -- unclaim, then reclaim to a different number, is the reassign flow, with no separate "reassign" action needed (SOLID: reuse existing claim logic, add only the missing primitive). One accepted cosmetic trade-off, documented in the code rather than silently glossed over: a business that was always MuniServe-native (never a real paper import) and gets unclaimed this way will show as "Legacy — not claimed" afterward, which isn't literally true -- this schema has no other vocabulary for "ownerless, claimable" today, and this is expected to be a rare admin-correction path, not worth a new column just for one label's accuracy.

Blocks unclaiming while any of the business's own applications are still in flight (exported `TERMINAL_STATUSES` from `business-status.ts`, reused rather than a second hardcoded list) -- a live application doesn't itself depend on `businesses.owner_id` for its own review workflow (every action keys off `application_id`), but the applicant's own `/status/[reference]` page DOES gate on `business.owner_id === the signed-in session's ownerId`, so unclaiming mid-review would silently lock the real applicant out of their own already-in-progress application's status page. New "Unlink owner" button (tone `bad`, matching Reject's own styling) next to the existing phone-update form on the Business Registry's expanded row. New `business_unclaimed` audit action.

**Verified against real production data**: built two synthetic claimed businesses at San Miguel -- one with only a `released` (terminal) application, one with a `pending_dept_review` (in-flight) application. A temporary, self-cleaning fixture route (deleted immediately after) replicated the action's exact guard-then-write logic via service-role. Confirmed: the terminal-only business unclaimed successfully (`owner_id` → null, `is_legacy_unclaimed` → true), and its resulting state was independently checked against `claimLegacyBusiness`'s own real guard condition to confirm the reassign loop genuinely closes, not just that the write succeeded; the in-flight business was correctly blocked with its owner untouched; and re-running unclaim on the now-ownerless business was correctly rejected with "no owner to unlink" rather than erroring oddly. All synthetic rows deleted afterward -- confirmed zero leftover data in production.

This closes the last item from the original 23-finding full-pipeline audit (2026-08-17) -- all 3 high-severity findings, all 13 medium, and all 7 low-severity findings are now addressed.

---

## 7rr. BPLO dashboard: the 8 pipeline stat cards double as tabs (2026-08-17)
